\eqref{eq:root-parameters}; expansion shows that its coefficients
are nonnegative, and the same theorem makes $C$ TN. Since
$P(0)=t_{-q}>0$, every nonpositive root is strictly negative, and
$t_p>0$ makes the degree exactly $p+q$. This proves that condition (1) holds if and only if
condition (3) holds, including repeated roots.

\emph{An upper--lower product with the prescribed symbol.}
Assume \eqref{eq:root-parameters} and use the shift from
Subsection~\ref{sec:limit}. There is the exact positive factorization
\begin{equation}\label{eq:positive-ul-toeplitz}
 T=t_{-q}
 \prod_{\nu=1}^{q}(\Lambda+\beta_\nu I)
 \prod_{\nu=q+1}^{p+q}(I+\beta_\nu\Lambda^{\mathsf T}).
\end{equation}
To see the identity, expand the first block in powers of $\Lambda$
and the second in powers of $\Lambda^{\mathsf T}$. Each resulting
monomial has all upper shifts on the left. The identity
$\Lambda\Lambda^{\mathsf T}=I$ therefore gives
\[
 \Lambda^r(\Lambda^{\mathsf T})^s=
 \begin{cases}
  \Lambda^{r-s},&r\ge s,\\
  (\Lambda^{\mathsf T})^{s-r},&s\ge r.
 \end{cases}
\]
Under the correspondence $\Lambda\leftrightarrow z^{-1}$ and
$\Lambda^{\mathsf T}\leftrightarrow z$, the expanded product thus
has Laurent polynomial
\[
 t_{-q}\prod_{\nu=1}^{q}(z^{-1}+\beta_\nu)
       \prod_{\nu=q+1}^{p+q}(1+\beta_\nu z)
 =z^{-q}P(z)=\sum_{k=-q}^{p}t_kz^k.
\]
Its coefficient $t_k$ occupies the diagonal $i-j=k$, which proves
\eqref{eq:positive-ul-toeplitz} entry by entry.

\emph{Positive exchanges and diagonal normalization.}
Every factor in the first group is positive upper
bidiagonal and every factor in the second is positive
unit lower bidiagonal. Fix the following exchange procedure: take the
lower factors in their original left-to-right order and move each
across all upper factors to its left, starting with the nearest one,
using Lemma~\ref{lem:exchange}. The relative order within each group
is preserved. Call an upper--lower inversion a pair formed by an
upper factor and a lower factor with the upper one to the left.
Each exchange decreases the number of such inversions by one.
Initially this number is $pq$;
after $pq$ exchanges all lower factors are on the left.
All parameters remain positive at every row.
The resulting upper factors may have nonunit diagonal. Writing
each as a positive diagonal times a unit upper bidiagonal and
commuting positive diagonals to the left by diagonal conjugation
gives \eqref{eq:strict-definition}, including the scalar $t_{-q}$
in $D$. This constructs the PBF in condition (4) from condition (3),
using exactly $pq$ exchanges.

\emph{Uniform bounds and the remaining implications.}
All exchanged upper factors have superdiagonal
$1$. If $u_n$ and $\ell_n$ have positive lower bounds
$u_-,\ell_-$ and finite upper bounds $u_+,\ell_+$, then
\[
 \ell_-<d_n\le u_++\ell_+,\qquad
 \frac{u_-\ell_-}{u_++\ell_+}\le L'_{n,n-1}<u_+.
\]
The upper bound on $d_n$ follows by dropping the nonnegative
subtracted term in \eqref{eq:swap-diagonal}; its lower bound is the
stronger inequality proved in Lemma~\ref{lem:exchange}. The bounds
on $L'_{n,n-1}=u_n\ell_n/d_{n-1}$ follow from these same inequalities.
They propagate through the finitely many exchanges, starting from
the positive constants $\beta_\nu$.
For diagonal normalization, a positive sequence bounded above and
away from zero has an inverse with the same properties. Finite
products of such sequences and their adjacent-entry ratios retain
these bounds, so extracting and commuting the diagonal factors
preserves boundedness and positive lower bounds.

Finally, a PBF implies BTP by Proposition~\ref{prop:positive-product};
hence condition (4) implies
condition (2). A BTP matrix is TN because its admissible minors are
positive and its other minors are zero, so condition (2) implies
condition (1). Together with the implications already proved, this
completes the equivalence.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- remark-15: body_md -->
The product in \eqref{eq:positive-ul-toeplitz} is in upper--lower
order, where $\Lambda\Lambda^{\mathsf T}=I$.
In the reverse order,
$\Lambda^{\mathsf T}\Lambda=I-e_0e_0^{\mathsf T}$.
The positive exchanges account for this initial-row term and give
a lower--diagonal--upper PBF whose bidiagonal entries generally vary
with the row index.
<!-- end remark-15 -->
<!-- remark-16: body_md -->
The negative-root condition characterizes simultaneous BTP of all
leading sections of a semi-infinite Toeplitz matrix.
At a fixed size, Theorem~\ref{thm:finite} characterizes BTP by
existence of a PBF. For example,
\[
 \begin{bmatrix}3/2&1\\1&3/2\end{bmatrix}
\]
is totally positive and admits a PBF, while
$1+(3/2)z+z^2$ has nonreal conjugate roots.
<!-- end remark-16 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:ordered-boundary-flag}
Let $H\in\mathbb R^{q\times q}$ and $K\in\mathbb R^{p\times p}$ be the matrices
defined by \eqref{eq:raw-H-definition}--\eqref{eq:normalized-boundary-definition}.
For
$n\in\{0,\dots,q-1\}$ and $b\in\{1,\dots,q\}$, put $r=n-b+1$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:H-general-inverse}
 (H^{-1})_{n,b-1}=
 \begin{cases}
 0,&n<b-1,\\
 (-1)^rh_r(\eta_{q-b+1},\ldots,\eta_q,
                    \xi_1,\ldots,\xi_p),&n\ge b-1.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reversed-root-data}
 P^\vee(z):=z^mP(1/z),\qquad
 \omega^\vee:=t_p=\omega\prod_{i=1}^q\eta_i
                              \prod_{j=1}^p\xi_j,
\end{equation}
and
\[
 \delta_i=\xi_{p+1-i}^{-1}\quad(i\in\{1,\dots,p\}),
 \qquad
 \widehat\eta_j=\eta_{q+1-j}^{-1}\quad(j\in\{1,\dots,q\}).
\]
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:P-reversed-factorization}
 P^\vee(z)=\omega^\vee
 \prod_{i=1}^{p}(1+\delta_i z)
 \prod_{j=1}^{q}(1+\widehat\eta_j z),
\end{equation}
and, for $a\in\{1,\dots,p\}$, $n\in\{0,\dots,p-1\}$, and $r=n-a+1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:K-general-inverse}
 (K^{-1})_{a-1,n}=
 \begin{cases}
 0,&n<a-1,\\
 (-1)^rh_r(\delta_{p-a+1},\ldots,\delta_p,
                    \widehat\eta_1,\ldots,\widehat\eta_q),&n\ge a-1.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
In particular, $H^{-1}$ is lower triangular and $K^{-1}$ is upper
triangular, both with diagonal entries equal to one.
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
\emph{A sequence satisfying the homogeneous equation for a partial product.}
For $v=[v_0,v_1,\ldots]^{\mathsf T}$, write
$\mathcal Gv(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb N_0}v_nz^n$. The elementary actions are
\begin{align}
 \mathcal G((I+\xi\Lambda^{\mathsf T})v)(z)&=(1+\xi z)\mathcal Gv(z),
 \label{eq:GF-lower-action}\\
 \mathcal G((\Lambda+\eta I)v)(z)
 &=\frac{(1+\eta z)\mathcal Gv(z)-v_0}{z}.
 \label{eq:GF-upper-action}
\end{align}
For $b\in\{1,\dots,q\}$, let $v^{(b)}$ have generating function
\begin{equation}\label{eq:zero-flag-vector}
 \mathcal Gv^{(b)}(z)=
 \frac{z^{b-1}}
 {\prod_{i=q-b+1}^{q}(1+\eta_i z)
  \prod_{j=1}^{p}(1+\xi_jz)}.
\end{equation}
Its first nonzero entry has index $b-1$ and equals one. Applying
all $p$ lower factors cancels the second product in the denominator,
leaving $z^{b-1}/\prod_{i=q-b+1}^{q}(1+\eta_i z)$.
After the rightmost $t$ upper factors have acted, for
$t\in\{0,\dots,b-1\}$, the generating function is
\[
 \frac{z^{b-1-t}}{\prod_{i=q-b+1}^{q-t}(1+\eta_i z)}.
\]
For $t<b-1$ its constant coefficient is zero, so
\eqref{eq:GF-upper-action} cancels one denominator factor and lowers
the power of $z$ by one. At $t=b-1$ the function is
$(1+\eta_{q-b+1}z)^{-1}$, whose constant coefficient is one; the
last upper factor therefore sends it to zero. Consequently
\[
 \mathcal U^{(0)}_{q-b+1}\cdots\mathcal U^{(0)}_q
 \mathcal L^{(0)}_1\cdots\mathcal L^{(0)}_p v^{(b)}=0.
\]
\emph{Preservation of this equation under the exchanges.}
Fix $b$, and let $V_j^{(b)}$ be the product of the rightmost $b$
upper factors after the first $j$ lower factors have been moved
entirely to the left. We prove inductively that
\[
 V_j^{(b)}\mathcal L_{j+1}^{(0)}\cdots
 \mathcal L_p^{(0)}v^{(b)}=0,\qquad j\in\{0,\dots,p\}.
\]
The case $j=0$ is the homogeneous equation just established.
At step $j$, the factor $\mathcal L_j^{(0)}$ first crosses the
rightmost $b$ upper factors. The $b$ exchange identities combine to
give
\[
 V_{j-1}^{(b)}\mathcal L_j^{(0)}
 =\widetilde{\mathcal L}_j V_j^{(b)}.
\]
The lower bidiagonal factor $\widetilde{\mathcal L}_j$ is formally
invertible: its diagonal entries are nonzero, and each row of its
inverse has only finitely many entries. It can therefore be
cancelled from that identity, proving the induction step.
The remaining exchanges move it past the other upper factors and
preserve the rightmost $b$ upper factors. At $j=p$ this yields
$V_p^{(b)}v^{(b)}=0$.

Diagonal normalization can be performed from right to left. For
each $b$ it writes $V_p^{(b)}=E_bU_b\cdots U_1$, with $E_b$
an invertible positive diagonal matrix. Indeed, after normalizing
the rightmost $b-1$ factors, one absorbs their extracted diagonal
into the next upper factor and normalizes that product. Hence
$U_b\cdots U_1v^{(b)}=0$ for the final unit upper factors.

\emph{Identification with the inverse initial-condition matrix.}
An upper product of $b$ bidiagonal factors has positive $b$th
superdiagonal. In its equation at row $n$, the coefficient of
$v_{n+b}$ is therefore nonzero. Prescribing
$v_0=\cdots=v_{b-2}=0$ and $v_{b-1}=1$ determines all remaining
entries recursively. Thus $v^{(b)}$ is the unique sequence satisfying this homogeneous equation
with those initial entries.

Let $w^{(b)}=[v_0^{(b)},\ldots,v_{q-1}^{(b)}]^{\mathsf T}$.
Row $i$ of $\widetilde H$ is the zeroth row of $U_i\cdots U_1$,
which uses only vector entries through index $i$. For $i<b-1$ its
product with $w^{(b)}$ vanishes. At $i=b-1$ the product is
$\widetilde H_{b-1,b-1}$, since the only nonzero contributing initial
entry is $v_{b-1}^{(b)}=1$. For $i\ge b$, the product vanishes
because $U_i\cdots U_1$ has $U_b\cdots U_1$ as its rightmost
subproduct. After row normalization, $Hw^{(b)}=e_{b-1}$. This
proves that $w^{(b)}$ is the column of $H^{-1}$ indexed by $b-1$.
Expanding the denominator in \eqref{eq:zero-flag-vector} by
\eqref{eq:h-reciprocal}, and accounting for the factor $z^{b-1}$,
gives precisely \eqref{eq:H-general-inverse}.

\emph{The left initial conditions directly from row sequences.}
For a row sequence $w=[w_0,w_1,\ldots]$, use the same generating
function convention $\mathcal Gw(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb N_0}w_nz^n$.
Right multiplication gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathcal G\bigl(w(\Lambda+\eta I)\bigr)(z)
   &=(\eta+z)\mathcal Gw(z),&
 \mathcal G\bigl(w(I+\xi\Lambda^{\mathsf T})\bigr)(z)
   &=\frac{(z+\xi)\mathcal Gw(z)-\xi w_0}{z}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Fix $a\in\{1,\dots,p\}$ and define $w^{(a)}$ by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:left-zero-flag-vector}
 \mathcal Gw^{(a)}(z)=
 \frac{z^{a-1}}
 {\prod_{i=1}^{q}(1+\eta_i^{-1}z)
  \prod_{j=1}^{a}(1+\xi_j^{-1}z)}.
\end{equation}
Its first nonzero entry is $w^{(a)}_{a-1}=1$.
Multiplication by all initial upper factors cancels the first
product in the denominator, with scalar multiplier
$\prod_{i=1}^q\eta_i$. After the first $t$ lower factors, for
$t\in\{0,\dots,a-1\}$, the generating function is
\[
 \left(\prod_{i=1}^q\eta_i\right)
 \left(\prod_{j=1}^t\xi_j\right)
 \frac{z^{a-1-t}}{\prod_{j=t+1}^{a}(1+\xi_j^{-1}z)}.
\]
At the last step the constant-term subtraction sends this sequence
to zero. Therefore
\[
 w^{(a)}\mathcal U^{(0)}_1\cdots\mathcal U^{(0)}_q
             \mathcal L^{(0)}_1\cdots\mathcal L^{(0)}_a=0.
\]
After the first $a$ rounds of the prescribed exchanges, their product
identity is
\[
 \mathcal U^{(0)}_1\cdots\mathcal U^{(0)}_q
 \mathcal L^{(0)}_1\cdots\mathcal L^{(0)}_a
 =L_1\cdots L_a\,\mathcal V^{(a)},
\]
where $\mathcal V^{(a)}$ is the current upper product, with positive
diagonal. It is injective for right multiplication on formal row
sequences: in $h\mathcal V^{(a)}=0$, column zero gives $h_0=0$,
and successive columns then give $h_n=0$ for every $n\ge1$.
It follows that $w^{(a)}L_1\cdots L_a=0$.
Later exchanges leave these first $a$ lower factors unchanged.
Diagonal normalization of the final upper block extracts its diagonal
only as far as the position between the lower and upper blocks, and
therefore also leaves them unchanged. This proves the homogeneous
equation for the actual lower factors used to define $K$.

Let $\widehat w^{(a)}=[w^{(a)}_0,\ldots,w^{(a)}_{p-1}]$.
Column $j$ of $\widetilde K$ is column zero of $L_1\cdots L_j$,
with support in rows indexed by $\{0,\dots,j\}$. Its pairing with
$\widehat w^{(a)}$ is zero for $j<a-1$, equals
$\widetilde K_{a-1,a-1}$ for $j=a-1$, and is zero for $j\ge a$
because $w^{(a)}L_1\cdots L_a=0$. Column normalization consequently
gives $\widehat w^{(a)}K=e_{a-1}^{\mathsf T}$.
Thus $\widehat w^{(a)}$ is the row of $K^{-1}$ indexed by $a-1$.
The multisets $(\xi_1^{-1},\ldots,\xi_a^{-1})$ and
$(\delta_{p-a+1},\ldots,\delta_p)$ agree, as do the reciprocal
upper parameters and $(\widehat\eta_1,\ldots,\widehat\eta_q)$.
Expanding \eqref{eq:left-zero-flag-vector} by
\eqref{eq:h-reciprocal} proves \eqref{eq:K-general-inverse} directly.
The factorization of $P^\vee$ in
\eqref{eq:P-reversed-factorization} follows by reversing the factors
of $P$ and extracting their positive leading coefficients.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{thm:general-toeplitz-polynomials}
The solutions of $A(x)T=xA(x)$ and $TB(x)=xB(x)$ with
\eqref{eq:general-initial-polynomials} are determined by
\begin{align}
 \sum_{n\in\mathbb N_0}B_n^{(b)}(x)z^n
 &=\frac{\omega z^{b-1}\prod_{i=1}^{q-b}(1+\eta_i z)}
 {P(z)-xz^q},&&b\in\{1,\dots,q\},
 \label{eq:general-right-GF}\\
 \sum_{n\in\mathbb N_0}A_n^{(a)}(x)z^n
 &=\frac{\omega^\vee z^{a-1}
                  \prod_{i=1}^{p-a}(1+\delta_i z)}
 {P^\vee(z)-xz^p},&&a\in\{1,\dots,p\}.
 \label{eq:general-left-GF}
\end{align}
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Denote the first quotient by $F_b(z;x)$. Its denominator has
nonzero constant term $\omega$, so it defines a formal power series
in $z$ with polynomial coefficients in $x$. At $x=0$ it is
\[
 \frac{z^{b-1}}
 {\prod_{i=q-b+1}^{q}(1+\eta_i z)
  \prod_{j=1}^{p}(1+\xi_jz)},
\]
whose first $q$ coefficients form the column of $H^{-1}$ indexed by
$b-1$, by Lemma~\ref{lem:ordered-boundary-flag}. The dependence on $x$
first enters at a power of $z$ at least $q$, so these initial
coefficients are unchanged for arbitrary $x$.

The identity
\[
 (P(z)-xz^q)F_b(z;x)
 =\omega z^{b-1}\prod_{i=1}^{q-b}(1+\eta_i z)
\]
has a right-hand side of degree $q-1$. For $n\in\mathbb N_0$, its coefficient
of $z^{n+q}$ is therefore zero. Since
$P(z)=\sum_{k=-q}^{p}t_kz^{k+q}$, the corresponding coefficient on
the left gives
\[
 \sum_{k=-q}^{p}t_kB_{n-k}^{(b)}(x)=xB_n^{(b)}(x),
 \qquad B_j^{(b)}=0\quad(j<0).
\]
This is the component form of $TB=xB$. The term $t_{-q}B_{n+q}^{(b)}$
has nonzero coefficient, so the recurrence and the first $q$ values
determine every later coefficient uniquely.

For the second quotient, the constant term of $P^\vee$ is
$\omega^\vee=t_p$. Its value at $x=0$ is
\[
 \frac{z^{a-1}}
 {\prod_{i=p-a+1}^{p}(1+\delta_i z)
  \prod_{j=1}^{q}(1+\widehat\eta_j z)}.
\]
The first $p$ coefficients are the row of $K^{-1}$ indexed by $a-1$.
They are independent of $x$. Now
$P^\vee(z)=\sum_{k=-q}^{p}t_kz^{p-k}$, and the numerator of the
second quotient has degree $p-1$. Comparison of the coefficient of
$z^{n+p}$ consequently gives
\[
 \sum_{k=-q}^{p}t_kA_{n+k}^{(a)}(x)=xA_n^{(a)}(x),
 \qquad A_j^{(a)}=0\quad(j<0).
\]
This is $AT=xA$, with $t_pA_{n+p}^{(a)}$ determining the next value.
Thus both displayed generating functions have the required initial
conditions and solve their respective recurrences.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- corollary-5: body_md -->
\label{cor:general-toeplitz-coefficients}
For $b\in\{1,\dots,q\}$ and $k\in\mathbb N_0$, let $\mathcal X_{b,k}$ be the list
containing $k$ copies of each of $\eta_1,\ldots,\eta_{q-b}$ and
$k+1$ copies of each of $\eta_{q-b+1},\ldots,\eta_q,\xi_1,\ldots,\xi_p$.
Explicitly,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Xbk}
\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal X_{b,k}:=\bigl(&
 \underbrace{\eta_1,\ldots,\eta_1}_{k},\ldots,
 \underbrace{\eta_{q-b},\ldots,\eta_{q-b}}_{k},
 \underbrace{\eta_{q-b+1},\ldots,\eta_{q-b+1}}_{k+1},\ldots,
 \underbrace{\eta_q,\ldots,\eta_q}_{k+1},\\
 &\underbrace{\xi_1,\ldots,\xi_1}_{k+1},\ldots,
 \underbrace{\xi_p,\ldots,\xi_p}_{k+1}\bigr).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Blocks of multiplicity zero and empty index ranges are omitted.
Then $B_n^{(b)}=0$ for $n<b-1$, while otherwise
\begin{equation}\label{eq:general-B-coefficients}
 B_n^{(b)}(x)=
 \sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor(n-b+1)/q\rfloor}
 (-1)^{N_{n,b,k}}\omega^{-k}
 h_{N_{n,b,k}}(\mathcal X_{b,k})x^k,
 \quad N_{n,b,k}=n-b+1-qk.
\end{equation}
For $a\in\{1,\dots,p\}$, define $\mathcal Z_{a,k}$ analogously:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Zak}
\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal Z_{a,k}:=\bigl(&
 \underbrace{\delta_1,\ldots,\delta_1}_{k},\ldots,
 \underbrace{\delta_{p-a},\ldots,\delta_{p-a}}_{k},
 \underbrace{\delta_{p-a+1},\ldots,\delta_{p-a+1}}_{k+1},\ldots,
 \underbrace{\delta_p,\ldots,\delta_p}_{k+1},\\
 &\underbrace{\widehat\eta_1,\ldots,\widehat\eta_1}_{k+1},\ldots,
 \underbrace{\widehat\eta_q,\ldots,\widehat\eta_q}_{k+1}\bigr).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Thus the first $p-a$ variables have multiplicity $k$, and all the
remaining variables have multiplicity $k+1$.
Then $A_n^{(a)}=0$ for $n<a-1$, while otherwise
\begin{equation}\label{eq:general-A-coefficients}
 A_n^{(a)}(x)=
 \sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor(n-a+1)/p\rfloor}
 (-1)^{M_{n,a,k}}(\omega^\vee)^{-k}
 h_{M_{n,a,k}}(\mathcal Z_{a,k})x^k,
 \quad M_{n,a,k}=n-a+1-pk.
\end{equation}
Every nonzero component has degree given by the corresponding upper
summation limit.
<!-- end corollary-5 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Expand the first generating function as a geometric series in
$xz^q/P(z)$. For a fixed $k\in\mathbb N_0$, its contribution is
\[
 \frac{\omega^{-k}x^kz^{qk+b-1}}
 {\prod_{i=1}^{q-b}(1+\eta_i z)^k
  \prod_{i=q-b+1}^{q}(1+\eta_i z)^{k+1}
  \prod_{j=1}^{p}(1+\xi_j z)^{k+1}}.
\]
The three products explain the multiplicities in $\mathcal X_{b,k}$.
By \eqref{eq:h-reciprocal}, the coefficient still needed to obtain
$z^n$ is
$(-1)^{N_{n,b,k}}h_{N_{n,b,k}}(\mathcal X_{b,k})$, where
$N_{n,b,k}=n-b+1-qk$. It vanishes when $N_{n,b,k}<0$, which gives
exactly the upper limit in \eqref{eq:general-B-coefficients}.

For the left generating function the contribution of $x^k$ is
\[
 \frac{(\omega^\vee)^{-k}x^kz^{pk+a-1}}
 {\prod_{i=1}^{p-a}(1+\delta_i z)^k
  \prod_{i=p-a+1}^{p}(1+\delta_i z)^{k+1}
  \prod_{j=1}^{q}(1+\widehat\eta_j z)^{k+1}}.
\]
Its remaining coefficient has index $M_{n,a,k}=n-a+1-pk$ and is
$(-1)^{M_{n,a,k}}h_{M_{n,a,k}}(\mathcal Z_{a,k})$.
This proves \eqref{eq:general-A-coefficients}, including its range.
At the largest allowed $k$, the complete homogeneous polynomial is
strictly positive: all its variables are positive, and its value
at index zero is one. The highest displayed power of $x$ therefore
has a nonzero coefficient in each nonzero polynomial.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- corollary-6: body_md -->
\label{prop:general-favard}
Let $T$ be a bounded semi-infinite banded matrix with a PBF
$T=L_1\cdots L_pDU_q\cdots U_1$ whose factors are bounded.
Define $H,K$ from its partial products by
\eqref{eq:raw-H-definition}--\eqref{eq:normalized-boundary-definition}.
Let $B_n$ be the row polynomial solutions of $TB=xB$, with the
matrix of first $q$ rows equal to $H^{-1}$, and let $A_n$ be the
column polynomial solutions of $AT=xA$, with the matrix of first
$p$ columns equal to $K^{-1}$.
There is a unique $q\times p$ matrix $\Psi$ of entrywise positive,
compactly supported finite measures such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:general-moments}
 \int_{\mathbb R} x^\ell\,\mathrm d\Psi(x)=HE_qT^\ell E_p^{\mathsf T}K,
 \qquad \ell\in\mathbb N_0,
\end{equation}
where $E_r=[I_r\ 0]$. It satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:general-biorthogonality}
 \int_{\mathbb R} B_n(x)\,\mathrm d\Psi(x)\,A_l(x)=\delta_{n,l}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-6 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
The normalized first columns of $L_1\cdots L_j$ and first rows of
$U_i\cdots U_1$ give the two triangular matrices used in the Favard
construction. We divide each column or row by its diagonal entry,
as in \eqref{eq:normalized-boundary-definition}. This is the unit-diagonal
normalization stated in \cite[Lemma~7.10]{BFM2023} and used in the
proof of its Theorem~7.11. The upper-factor order follows the right
eigenvector transformation in \cite[Lemma~7.8(iii)]{BFM2023}.
Definition~7.9 there prints that order in reverse and gives normalizing
constants with one extra factor; the diagonal-entry normalization
above resolves both discrepancies.

For clarity, attach the subscript $\mathrm{BFM}$ to the initial-condition
symbols of \cite[Theorem~9.1]{BFM2023}. Its hypotheses are met with
$\nu_{\mathrm{BFM}}^{-\mathsf T}=K$,
$\xi_{\mathrm{BFM}}^{-1}=H$, identity auxiliary matrices of orders
$p,q$, and shift $s=0$. Boundedness of $T$ and positivity
of its factor entries verify the remaining hypotheses. That theorem
gives the compactly supported positive measures and
\eqref{eq:general-biorthogonality}; the moment representation
\cite[Proposition~9.4 and Remark~9.5]{BFM2023} is exactly
\eqref{eq:general-moments}.

For uniqueness, the difference of two compactly supported measures
with these moments annihilates every polynomial. Polynomial density
in the continuous functions on a compact interval containing both
supports makes this difference zero, entry by entry. The same argument
applies to finite signed measures.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- corollary-7: body_md -->
\label{prop:change-normalization}
Suppose two bounded PBFs of the same bounded matrix $T$ give initial
matrices $(H,K)$ and $(H',K')$ as in
Corollary~\ref{prop:general-favard}. Denote their measures by
$\Psi,\Psi'$ and their polynomial families by $(B_n,A_n)$ and
$(B'_n,A'_n)$. Then, as matrices of measures and polynomial vectors,
\begin{align}
 \mathrm d\Psi'&=H'H^{-1}\,\mathrm d\Psi\,K^{-1}K',
 \label{eq:change-measure}\\
 B'_n&=B_nHH'^{-1},\qquad
 A'_n=K'^{-1}KA_n,\qquad n\in\mathbb N_0.
 \label{eq:change-polynomials}
\end{align}
<!-- end corollary-7 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
The matrix of signed measures $H'H^{-1}\,\mathrm d\Psi\,K^{-1}K'$
has moments $H'E_qT^\ell E_p^{\mathsf T}K'$, hence equals $\mathrm d\Psi'$
by compact-support uniqueness. The polynomial transformations in
\eqref{eq:change-polynomials} preserve the recurrences and give
initial matrices $H'^{-1}$ and $K'^{-1}$. The nonzero extreme
diagonals determine each subsequent vector uniquely.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- corollary-8: body_md -->
\label{cor:general-step}
For $r\in\mathbb N$ and $n\in\mathbb N_0$, define $\nu_r(n)\in\mathbb N_0^r$ by writing $n=rk+s$,
$s\in\{0,\dots,r-1\}$, and setting its first $s$ entries equal to $k+1$ and
the remaining entries equal to $k$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:general-step-degrees}
 \deg B_n^{(b)}=\nu_q(n+1)_b-1,
 \qquad
 \deg A_n^{(a)}=\nu_p(n+1)_a-1,
\end{equation}
where degree $-1$ denotes the zero polynomial. The polynomial families of
Theorem~\ref{thm:general-toeplitz-polynomials} satisfy
\begin{align}
 \sum_{b=1}^q\int_{\mathbb R} x^j B_n^{(b)}(x)\,\mathrm d\Psi_{b,a}(x)&=0,
 &&j\in\{0,\dots,\nu_p(n)_a-1\},\quad a\in\{1,\dots,p\},
 \label{eq:general-mixed-right}\\
 \sum_{a=1}^p\int_{\mathbb R} x^j A_n^{(a)}(x)\,\mathrm d\Psi_{b,a}(x)&=0,
 &&j\in\{0,\dots,\nu_q(n)_b-1\},\quad b\in\{1,\dots,q\}.
 \label{eq:general-mixed-left}
\end{align}
Their common biorthogonal normalization is
\eqref{eq:general-biorthogonality}.
<!-- end corollary-8 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
Corollary~\ref{cor:general-toeplitz-coefficients} gives the stated
degrees and nonzero highest coefficients. Use $e_0,\ldots,e_{p-1}$ for the coordinate columns in $\mathbb R^p$.
Order the column monomials as
\[
 e_0,\ldots,e_{p-1},
 xe_0,\ldots,xe_{p-1},
 x^2e_0,\ldots.
\]
The vector $A_j$ contains only the first $j+1$ monomials in this
list, with a nonzero coefficient of the last one. The coefficient
matrix of $A_0,\ldots,A_{n-1}$ in the first $n$ monomials is
therefore triangular with nonzero diagonal. These vectors form a
basis of
\[
 \operatorname{span}\{x^je_{a-1}:
  a\in\{1,\dots,p\},\quad j\in\{0,\dots,\nu_p(n)_a-1\}\}.
\]
Biorthogonality of $B_n$ against $A_0,\ldots,A_{n-1}$ is thus
equivalent to \eqref{eq:general-mixed-right}.
For the other relation, use the row coordinate vectors
$e_{b-1}^{\mathsf T}\in\mathbb R^{1\times q}$, ordered by increasing
degree and then by $b$. The same triangular argument shows that
$B_0,\ldots,B_{n-1}$ span the first $n$ row monomials, proving
\eqref{eq:general-mixed-left}. The pairing at equal indices is one
by \eqref{eq:general-biorthogonality}.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{thm:general-resolvent}
For $|\lambda|$ sufficiently large,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:general-R}
 [ (\lambda I-T)^{-1}]_{i,j}
 =\Gamma(\lambda)\sum_{k=0}^{\min(i,j)}
 h_{j-k}(S)h_{i-k}(\mathcal Z^{-1}).
\end{equation}
For any bounded PBF of this Toeplitz matrix, with its associated
$H,K$, the Stieltjes matrix of Corollary~\ref{prop:general-favard} is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:general-W}
 W(\lambda):=\int_{\mathbb R}\frac{\mathrm d\Psi(x)}{\lambda-x}
 =H[ (\lambda I-T)^{-1}_{i,j}]_{
       \substack{i\in\{0,\dots,q-1\}\\j\in\{0,\dots,p-1\}}}K.
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
For column $j$, let $F_j(z)=\sum_{i\in\mathbb N_0}[(\lambda I-T)^{-1}]_{i,j}z^i$.
For $|\lambda|>\|T\|_2$, each resolvent column belongs to $\ell^2$,
so $F_j$ is analytic for $|z|<1$. Increasing $|\lambda|$ if necessary
puts all roots in $S$ in this disk and all roots in $\mathcal Z$
outside it. Multiply the column equation
$(\lambda I-T)[(\lambda I-T)^{-1}]_{\cdot,j}=e_j$ by $z^{n+q}$
and sum over $n\in\mathbb N_0$. The omitted terms with negative row index
form a polynomial $N_j$ of degree less than $q$, giving
\[
 Q(z;\lambda)F_j(z)=N_j(z)-z^{q+j}.
\]
Analyticity of $F_j$ requires the numerator on the right to vanish
at every root in $S$, with its multiplicity. Hence $N_j$ is exactly
the remainder when $z^{q+j}$ is divided by
$\prod_{u\in S}(z-u)$.

To compute the quotient, expand at infinity:
\[
 \frac{z^{q+j}}{\prod_{u\in S}(z-u)}
 =z^j\prod_{u\in S}(1-u/z)^{-1}
 =\sum_{r\in\mathbb N_0}h_r(S)z^{j-r}.
\]
Its polynomial part is $\sum_{k=0}^{j}h_{j-k}(S)z^k$.
Cancellation of the factor corresponding to $S$ therefore gives
\[
 F_j(z)=-\frac{\sum_{k=0}^{j}h_{j-k}(S)z^k}
 {t_p\prod_{v\in\mathcal Z}(z-v)}.
\]
Since the constant term of $Q$ is $\omega$, its roots satisfy
$\prod_{u\in S}u\prod_{v\in\mathcal Z}v=(-1)^m\omega/t_p$.
Consequently
\[
 -\frac1{t_p\prod_{v\in\mathcal Z}(z-v)}
 =\Gamma(\lambda)\prod_{v\in\mathcal Z}(1-z/v)^{-1}
 =\Gamma(\lambda)\sum_{r\in\mathbb N_0}h_r(\mathcal Z^{-1})z^r.
\]
The coefficient of $z^i$ in its product with the quotient above
is the finite sum in \eqref{eq:general-R}.

Finally, the Neumann series for $(\lambda I-T)^{-1}$ and the
geometric expansion of $(\lambda-x)^{-1}$ converge for sufficiently
large $|\lambda|$. Their coefficients are, respectively, $T^\ell$
and the moments of $\Psi$. After selecting the first $q$ rows and
$p$ columns and multiplying by $H,K$, equation
\eqref{eq:general-moments} identifies those coefficients term by term.
This proves \eqref{eq:general-W}.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\label{thm:general-support}
There is a unique $z_+>0$ with $\mathcal F(z_+)=q$. Put
$\lambda_+=a(z_+)$. If $\beta_q>\beta_{q+1}$, there is a unique
\[
 z_-\in(-\beta_{q+1}^{-1},-\beta_q^{-1})
 \quad\hbox{with}\quad \mathcal F(z_-)=q,
\]
and put $\lambda_-=a(z_-)$. If
$\beta_q=\beta_{q+1}$, put $\lambda_-=0$.
The limiting set of eigenvalues of the finite Toeplitz sections is
$[\lambda_-,\lambda_+]$. For the measure associated with any bounded
PBF of this Toeplitz matrix,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:general-support-inclusion}
 \operatorname{supp}\Psi\subseteq[\lambda_-,\lambda_+].
\end{equation}
For each $x\in(\lambda_-,\lambda_+)$ the two middle roots of
$Q(z;x)$ form a nonreal conjugate pair
$\zeta(x),\overline{\zeta(x)}$, with $\operatorname{Im}\zeta(x)<0$, and they are the
only roots on their common modulus circle. There are exactly $q-1$
roots of smaller modulus and $p-1$ of larger modulus.
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
\emph{The critical points.}
On the positive real axis,
$\mathcal F'(z)=\sum_{\nu=1}^m\beta_\nu/(1+\beta_\nu z)^2>0$,
and $\mathcal F$ increases from $0$ to $m$. Since $0<q<m$, it takes
the value $q$ exactly once. On the negative interval specified in
the theorem, its derivative is again positive and its limits at the
left and right endpoints are $-\infty$ and $+\infty$. This gives
the stated existence and uniqueness of $z_-$. Logarithmic
differentiation of $a=z^{-q}P$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:a-critical}
 \frac{a'(z)}{a(z)}=\frac{\mathcal F(z)-q}{z}.
\end{equation}
For $z>0$, this derivative is negative before $z_+$ and positive
after $z_+$. Hence
$\lambda_+=a(z_+)=\min_{z>0}a(z)$.

The sign of $P(z_-)$ is $(-1)^q$, the same as that of $z_-^q$;
hence $\lambda_->0$ when the gap is strict. Also,
$a''(z_\pm)=a(z_\pm)\mathcal F'(z_\pm)/z_\pm$, so these critical
points are quadratic, with $a''(z_-)<0<a''(z_+)$.

\emph{The interval determined by equal root moduli.}
Let $r_+=z_+$. On $|z|=r_+$,
$|P(z)|\le P(r_+)=\lambda_+r_+^q$, with equality only at $z=r_+$.
For $x>\lambda_+$, the term $-xz^q$ strictly dominates $P$ on
this circle. Rouch\'e's theorem therefore gives exactly $q$ zeros
of $Q(z;x)$ inside it and $p$ outside it. In particular, the roots
in positions $q$ and $q+1$, ordered by increasing modulus, have
different moduli.
When the central gap is strict, let $r_-=-z_-$. Then
$\beta_q^{-1}<r_-<\beta_{q+1}^{-1}$ and, factor by factor,
\[
 |P(r_-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta})|\ge |P(-r_-)|=\lambda_-r_-^q,
\]
with equality only at $\theta=\pi$. For $0\le x<\lambda_-$,
the polynomial $P$ strictly dominates $xz^q$. It has exactly $q$
zeros inside the circle, namely $-\beta_1^{-1},\ldots,-\beta_q^{-1}$.
Rouch\'e again separates the first $q$ roots from the remaining $p$.
At $x=\lambda_+$ the two groups meet at the double root $z_+$;
at $x=\lambda_-$ they meet at the double root $z_-$ when the gap
is strict. Every other root lies strictly inside or outside the relevant
circle, by the strict modulus inequality away from that real point.
If $\beta_q=\beta_{q+1}$, the $q$th
and $(q+1)$st roots of $P=Q(\,\cdot\,;0)$ already have equal modulus.

Let $\Sigma$ be the set of complex $\lambda$ for which those two
ordered moduli are equal. The Schmidt--Spitzer theorem
\cite{SchmidtSpitzer} identifies $\Sigma$ with the limiting set of
eigenvalues of the finite Toeplitz sections. Those sections are
BTP by Theorem~\ref{thm:toeplitz}, and
Corollary~\ref{cor:btp-oscillatory} makes their eigenvalues positive
and simple; thus $\Sigma\subseteq[0,\infty)$. Ullman's theorem
\cite{Ullman} states that this set is connected. The preceding
root counts give $\Sigma\subseteq[\lambda_-,\lambda_+]$ and put
both endpoints in $\Sigma$. A connected subset
of the real line containing both endpoints contains the interval
between them. Hence $\Sigma=[\lambda_-,\lambda_+]$.

\emph{The roots inside the interval and the measure support.}
Fix $x\in(\lambda_-,\lambda_+)$. Since $x\in\Sigma$, the roots in
positions $q,q+1$ have a common modulus $r$. On any circle $|z|=r$,
\[
 |P(r\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta})|^2=\omega^2\prod_{\nu=1}^{m}
 (1+\beta_\nu^2r^2+2\beta_\nu r\cos\theta),
\]
which is strictly increasing in $\cos\theta$. At a root of $Q(z;x)$
on this circle its value must be $x^2r^{2q}$. Thus at most two
distinct roots can have modulus $r$, and, when nonreal, they are
conjugates. Every nonreal root is simple for $x>0$:
a multiple root would satisfy $\mathcal F(z)=q$, whereas
\[
 \operatorname{Im}\mathcal F(z)=\operatorname{Im} z\sum_{\nu=1}^{m}
                  \frac{\beta_\nu}{|1+\beta_\nu z|^2}
\]
is nonzero for nonreal $z$.

There is no positive root, since $a(z)\ge\lambda_+>x$ for $z>0$.
Two distinct negative roots cannot have the same modulus. Thus the
two middle roots are either a nonreal conjugate pair or a repeated
negative root $c$. In the latter case, $c$ is not a zero of $P$
because $x>0$, and \eqref{eq:a-critical} gives $\mathcal F(c)=q$.
Such a point must lie in a strict gap between consecutive distinct
roots of $P$: on $(-\infty,-\beta_m^{-1})$ each summand in
$\mathcal F$ is greater than $1$, so $\mathcal F>m>q$; on
$(-\beta_1^{-1},0)$ each summand is negative, so $\mathcal F<0<q$.
In each strict gap, $\mathcal F'>0$ and its endpoint limits are
$-\infty,+\infty$, so there is exactly one such critical point.

Consider that point $c$ in the $k$th strict gap and put
$x_*=a(c)>0$.
On the circle $|z|=-c$, the product formula gives
\[
 |P(z)|\ge |P(c)|=x_*|c|^q,
\]
with equality only at $z=c$. For $0<x<x_*$ sufficiently close to
$x_*$, Rouch\'e's theorem therefore counts exactly $k$ roots of
$Q(z;x)$ inside this circle. Moreover,
$a''(c)=a(c)\mathcal F'(c)/c<0$, so the two roots near $c$ are real
and have the form
\[
 c\pm\sqrt{\frac{2(x_*-x)}{-a''(c)}}
   +\mathrm O(x_*-x),\qquad x\uparrow x_*.
\]
One lies inside the circle and the other outside. No other root
is on the circle at $x=x_*$. Thus the colliding roots occupy
positions $k,k+1$ in increasing modulus. They are the middle roots
only when $k=q$, which gives $x_*=\lambda_-$, outside the open
interval under consideration. If $\beta_q=\beta_{q+1}$, there is
no strict gap with $k=q$, so no negative collision can involve the
middle roots at any $x>0$. This excludes the remaining real case.
The two middle roots are therefore nonreal and conjugate throughout
the open interval. They are simple and are the only roots on their
modulus circle, so exactly $q-1$ roots have smaller modulus and
$p-1$ have larger modulus.

The sums in \eqref{eq:general-R} are symmetric within each separated
root group. They therefore continue analytically off $\Sigma$ even
when roots within one group coincide, and their values are real on
the real complement of $\Sigma$. Applying Stieltjes inversion to
\eqref{eq:general-W} gives
\eqref{eq:general-support-inclusion}. This establishes the support
bound without using the density formula. The next theorem determines
the density and proves equality of the support with this interval.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- theorem-9: body_md -->
\label{thm:general-measure}
For any bounded PBF of the Toeplitz matrix under consideration, use
its initial matrices $H,K$ in \eqref{eq:general-density-data}.
The associated Favard measure is purely absolutely continuous, has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:general-support-interval}
 \operatorname{supp}\Psi=[\lambda_-,\lambda_+],
\end{equation}
and satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:general-weight}
 \mathrm d\Psi(x)=\rho(x)f(x)g(x)^{\mathsf T}\mathrm d x,
 \qquad \lambda_-<x<\lambda_+.
\end{equation}
Here $\rho>0$, while every entry of $f$ and $g$ is nonnegative. The
density has rank exactly one, and its upper-left entry is $\rho$.
<!-- end theorem-9 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
\emph{The jump of the Stieltjes transform.}
First sum \eqref{eq:general-R} over both indices. Reindexing its
finite inner sum by $i=k+r$ and $j=k+\ell$ separates the three sums
and gives, for formal variables $s,t$,
\[
 \sum_{i,j\in\mathbb N_0}[(\lambda I-T)^{-1}]_{i,j}s^it^j
 =\frac{\Gamma(\lambda)}{1-st}
  \prod_{u\in S}(1-ut)^{-1}
  \prod_{v\in\mathcal Z}(1-s/v)^{-1}.
\]
Across the interval, $\mathcal S_0$ and $\mathcal Z_0$ are unchanged,
while $\zeta$ and $\bar\zeta$ exchange groups. The elementary identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:general-jump-identity}
 \frac{\zeta}{(1-\zeta t)(1-s/\bar\zeta)}
 -\frac{\bar\zeta}{(1-\bar\zeta t)(1-s/\zeta)}
 =\frac{(\zeta-\bar\zeta)(1-st)}
 {(1-\zeta t)(1-\bar\zeta t)
  (1-s/\zeta)(1-s/\bar\zeta)}
\end{equation}
cancels the factor $(1-st)^{-1}$. The remaining generating function
is a product of a function of $s$ and a function of $t$. Its coefficients
give a matrix of rank one, with column
$[h_i(\widehat{\mathcal Z}^{-1})]$ and row $[h_j(\widehat{\mathcal S})]$.

To determine the scalar factor and its sign, observe that $\mathcal S_0$ is
invariant under conjugation. For $z>0$ and
$x\in(\lambda_-,\lambda_+)$, the minimum just established gives
$a(z)\ge\lambda_+>x$. Since $Q(0;x)=\omega>0$, every real root of
$Q(z;x)$ is therefore negative. Conjugate pairs in $\mathcal S_0$ have positive
products. As $\mathcal S_0$ contains $q-1$ roots,
\[
 \prod_{u\in \mathcal S_0}u=(-1)^{q-1}\prod_{u\in \mathcal S_0}|u|,
 \qquad
 \Gamma_+(x)=\frac{\zeta(x)}{\omega}\prod_{u\in \mathcal S_0}|u|.
\]
The lower boundary value is the conjugate of the upper one. Hence
the density contributed by the scalar jump is
$-(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{-1}(\Gamma_+-\Gamma_-)
=-\pi^{-1}\operatorname{Im}\Gamma_+=\rho>0$.
Premultiplication by $H$ and postmultiplication by $K$ turn the two
coefficient vectors into $f$ and $g^{\mathsf T}$.
Entrywise Stieltjes inversion now gives \eqref{eq:general-weight}
on the open interval.

\emph{Positivity, rank, and support.}
Since $H$ is lower triangular and $K$ is upper triangular, both with
diagonal entries equal to one, their first row and first column,
respectively, select the constant
complete homogeneous polynomial $h_0=1$. Thus the first components
of $f$ and $g$ are one. The measures supplied by Favard are positive,
so their density entries are nonnegative almost everywhere. Those
entries are continuous in the open interval, and are consequently
nonnegative at every point there. The first column of the density is
$\rho f$ and its first row is $\rho g^{\mathsf T}$; since $\rho>0$,
this proves $f,g\ge0$. Since $f_1=g_1=1$, both vectors are nonzero,
so the rank is exactly one. The first density entry
is strictly positive on the whole open interval. Every open subinterval
therefore has positive measure. Taking the closure and combining this
with \eqref{eq:general-support-inclusion} proves
\eqref{eq:general-support-interval}.

\emph{Absolute continuity on the full support.}
At a coincidence of roots inside one of the groups, the complete
homogeneous polynomials in \eqref{eq:general-R} are symmetric in
those roots, so the formula is invariant under permutation of their
individual branches. At a coincidence between the two groups, both limiting
formulas remain finite. Indeed, near any finite $\lambda_0$ the
coefficients of $Q$ are bounded and its leading and constant
coefficients are fixed and nonzero. Its roots are therefore uniformly
bounded and bounded away from zero. The finite sums in
\eqref{eq:general-R}, and hence every entry of $W$, remain bounded.

Let $[a,b]$ be a closed interval avoiding the finitely many algebraic
branch values. The branch of each entry of $W$ obtained from the
upper half-plane extends analytically to a neighborhood of this
interval. Thus $W(x+\mathrm i\epsilon)$ converges uniformly to
its boundary value on $[a,b]$ as $\epsilon\downarrow0$.
The Stieltjes inversion formula on subintervals then identifies
the whole restriction of the measure with the density
$-\pi^{-1}\operatorname{Im} W(x+\mathrm i0)$; no singular part remains there.
Any remaining singular measure must consequently be supported on
the finite set of branch values, and can only consist of atoms.
For each scalar entry and each real $x_0$, an atom is detected by
\[
 \Psi_{b,a}(\{x_0\})
 =\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow0}
       [-\epsilon\operatorname{Im} W_{b,a}(x_0+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)].
\]
Boundedness of $W$ makes this limit zero. The measure is therefore
purely absolutely continuous on its full support.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- remark-17: body_md -->
When $\beta_q>\beta_{q+1}$, the strict monotonicity of $\mathcal F$
permits bisection inside the negative interval in
Theorem~\ref{thm:general-support}. The resulting critical points
and endpoints satisfy
\[
 -\beta_{q+1}^{-1}<z_-<-\beta_q^{-1}<0<z_+,
 \qquad 0\le\lambda_-<\lambda_+.
\]
The case $\beta_q=\beta_{q+1}$ instead uses $\lambda_-=0$ directly.

If the roots are stored in increasing modulus with zero-based indices,
the central conjugate pair occupies indices $q-1,q$, and $\zeta$
is its member with negative imaginary part.
The smaller and larger sets contain exactly $q-1$ and $p-1$ roots.
For $q=1$ the smaller-root product in \eqref{eq:general-density-data}
is empty and equals one, giving
$\rho(x)=-\operatorname{Im}\zeta(x)/(\pi\omega)>0$.
<!-- end remark-17 -->
<!-- proposition-9: body_md -->
\label{prop:general-edges}
If $\beta_q>\beta_{q+1}$, let $\mathcal S_{0,-}$ and $\mathcal S_{0,+}$ be the limiting
$(q-1)$-root sets at the lower and upper endpoints. Then
\begin{align}
 \rho(x)&\sim
 \frac{\prod_{u\in \mathcal S_{0,-}}|u|}{\pi\omega}
 \sqrt{\frac{2}{-a''(z_-)}}\sqrt{x-\lambda_-},
 &&x\downarrow\lambda_-,\label{eq:general-lower-edge}\\
 \rho(x)&\sim
 \frac{\prod_{u\in \mathcal S_{0,+}}|u|}{\pi\omega}
 \sqrt{\frac{2}{a''(z_+)}}\sqrt{\lambda_+-x},
 &&x\uparrow\lambda_+ .\label{eq:general-upper-edge}
\end{align}
Write $C_-$ and $C_+$ for the positive constants preceding the
square roots in these two formulas. The corresponding matrix limits
of the density are
\[
 \frac{1}{\sqrt{x-\lambda_-}}\,\frac{\mathrm d\Psi}{\mathrm d x}(x)
 \longrightarrow C_-f_-g_-^{\mathsf T}
 \quad(x\downarrow\lambda_-),\qquad
 \frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_+-x}}\,\frac{\mathrm d\Psi}{\mathrm d x}(x)
 \longrightarrow C_+f_+g_+^{\mathsf T}
 \quad(x\uparrow\lambda_+).
\]
An entrywise asymptotic equivalent follows whenever the corresponding
entry of $f_\pm g_\pm^{\mathsf T}$ is nonzero. If it is zero, the
limit gives a smaller order and does not specify the first nonzero term.
If instead the root
$-\beta_q^{-1}$ belongs to a central block of multiplicity $d\ge2$,
then $\lambda_-=0$ and $\rho(x)\asymp x^{1/d}$ as $x\downarrow0$;
here $\asymp$ means that the ratio is bounded above and below by
positive constants near the stated limit. Individual matrix
entries can vanish to higher orders.
<!-- end proposition-9 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
At a critical point, differentiation of \eqref{eq:a-critical} gives
\[
 a''(z_*)=a(z_*)\frac{\mathcal F'(z_*)}{z_*}.
\]
Thus $a''(z_-)<0<a''(z_+)$. Taylor expansion at either point gives
\[
 a(z)=\lambda_\pm+\tfrac12 a''(z_\pm)(z-z_\pm)^2
       +\mathrm O((z-z_\pm)^3),\qquad z\to z_\pm.
\]
Choose in each case the root with negative imaginary part. Inverting
these expansions yields
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \zeta(x)&=z_--\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\frac{2}{-a''(z_-)}}
           \sqrt{x-\lambda_-}+\mathrm O(x-\lambda_-),
           &&x\downarrow\lambda_-,\\
 \zeta(x)&=z_+-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\frac{2}{a''(z_+)}}
           \sqrt{\lambda_+-x}+\mathrm O(\lambda_+-x),
           &&x\uparrow\lambda_+.
 \end{aligned}
\]
All roots in $\mathcal S_0$ have finite nonzero limits. Substitution into
\eqref{eq:general-density-data} proves the two scalar equivalents;
the limits of $f$ and $g$ give the stated matrix behavior.

For the multiple-root case write $z_0=-\beta_q^{-1}$ and
$a(z)=C(z-z_0)^d[1+\mathrm O(z-z_0)]$ as $z\to z_0$, with real
$C\ne0$. Let $s$ be the number of root parameters strictly larger
than $\beta_q$, and put $k=q-s\in\{1,\dots,d-1\}$. The sign of
$C$ is $(-1)^k$. The leading displacements of the $d$ roots have
modulus $|C|^{-1/d}x^{1/d}$ and arguments given by the $d$th roots
of $(-1)^k$. Ordering their moduli around the negative real point
$z_0$ selects the middle pair with arguments $\pm\pi k/d$.
In particular,
\[
 -\operatorname{Im}\zeta(x)\sim |C|^{-1/d}\sin(\pi k/d)x^{1/d},
 \qquad x\downarrow0.
\]
Here $\sin(\pi k/d)>0$. As $x\downarrow0$, the other factors in
$\rho$ have positive finite limits. Hence
$\rho(x)\asymp x^{1/d}$ as $x\downarrow0$. Some components
of $f$ or $g$ can tend to zero as $x\downarrow0$; their products with $\rho$ can
therefore have a higher order of vanishing.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- proposition-10: body_md -->
\label{prop:pbf}
Every finite leading section is BTP. The bounded PBF of $T$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pbf}
 T=L_q^{\,p}D(L_p^{\mathsf T})^{q}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-10 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
Write $L_q=I+N_q$, where $(N_q)_{n,n-1}=n/(n+q)$ and all other
entries vanish. The only nonzero entry of $N_q^r$ in row $i$ is
at column $i-r$, and it equals
\[
 \prod_{h=0}^{r-1}\frac{i-h}{i-h+q}
 =\frac{i!\,(i-r+q)!}{(i-r)!\,(i+q)!},
 \qquad r\in\{0,\dots,i\}.
\]
Expanding $(I+N_q)^p$, and applying the same calculation before
transposing the upper factor, gives
\begin{align*}
 (L_q^p)_{i,k}&=\binom p{i-k}
 \frac{i!\,(k+q)!}{k!\,(i+q)!},&
 ((L_p^{\mathsf T})^q)_{k,j}&=\binom q{j-k}
 \frac{j!\,(k+p)!}{k!\,(j+p)!}.
\end{align*}
After multiplying by $D_{k,k}$, the factors involving $k+p$ and
$k+q$ cancel. The entry $(i,j)$ of the proposed product becomes
\[
 \frac{m!\,i!\,j!}{(i+q)!\,(j+p)!}
 \sum_{k\in\mathbb N_0}\binom p{i-k}\binom q{j-k}\binom{m+k}{k}.
\]
It remains to use the identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:convolution}
 \sum_{k\in\mathbb N_0}\binom p{i-k}\binom q{j-k}\binom{m+k}{k}
 =\binom{i+q}{j}\binom{j+p}{i}.
\end{equation}
For the left-hand side, put $i=k+r$, $j=k+\ell$ and sum over
$i,j\in\mathbb N_0$. The resulting generating function is
\[
 \left(\sum_{r=0}^p\binom pr s^r\right)
 \left(\sum_{\ell=0}^q\binom q\ell t^\ell\right)
 \left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb N_0}\binom{m+k}{k}(st)^k\right)
 =\frac{(1+s)^p(1+t)^q}{(1-st)^{m+1}}.
\]
The right-hand side has the same generating function. One way to
check this last assertion using only Pascal's identity is to put
$c_{p,q}(i,j)=\binom{i+q}{j}\binom{j+p}{i}$, with value zero when
either index is negative. Pascal's identity gives
\[
 c_{p+1,q}(i,j)-c_{p+1,q}(i-1,j-1)
 =c_{p,q}(i,j)+c_{p,q}(i-1,j).
\]
For the generating functions this means multiplication by
$(1+s)/(1-st)$ when $p$ is increased by one. Interchanging
$(p,i,s)$ with $(q,j,t)$ gives multiplication by $(1+t)/(1-st)$
when $q$ is increased by one. The initial case is
$c_{0,0}(i,j)=\delta_{i,j}$, with generating function $(1-st)^{-1}$.
Induction on $p+q$ proves the assertion and hence
\eqref{eq:convolution}.

Substitution in the product entry above gives
$m!/[(q+i-j)!(p+j-i)!]=\binom m{q+i-j}$ whenever the indices lie
in the band, and gives zero otherwise. This proves the factorization.
The bidiagonal entries are between zero and one, and $D_{n,n}$ tends to
one as $n\to\infty$. The finite leading sections factor by the corresponding finite
factors: every summation index is at most $\min(i,j)$ in the
lower-diagonal-upper product. The implication $\mathrm{PBF}\Rightarrow\mathrm{BTP}$
then follows from Proposition~\ref{prop:positive-product}.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- theorem-10: body_md -->
\label{thm:polynomials}
The solutions of $AT=xA$ and $TB=xB$ with \eqref{eq:initial} are
\begin{align}
 B_n^{(b)}(x)
 &=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor(n-b+1)/q\rfloor}
 (-1)^{n-b+1-qk}
 \binom{n+p(k+1)}{n-b+1-qk}x^k,
 &&b\in\{1,\dots,q\},\label{eq:B}\\
 A_n^{(a)}(x)
 &=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor(n-a+1)/p\rfloor}
 (-1)^{n-a+1-pk}
 \binom{n+q(k+1)}{n-a+1-pk}x^k,
 &&a\in\{1,\dots,p\}.\label{eq:A}
\end{align}
A sum with negative upper limit is zero. Every nonzero component has
the degree indicated by the upper summation limit.
<!-- end theorem-10 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
For the right components the generating functions are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:GF}
 \sum_{n\in\mathbb N_0}B_n^{(b)}(x)z^n
 =\frac{z^{b-1}(1+z)^{q-b}}{(1+z)^m-xz^q}.
\end{equation}
Expanding the denominator first in $xz^q$ gives
\[
 \sum_{k\in\mathbb N_0}x^k z^{qk+b-1}(1+z)^{-mk-p-b},
\]
For a term with fixed $k$, set $r=n-b+1-qk$ and $M=mk+p+b$.
The negative-binomial expansion
\[
 [z^r](1+z)^{-M}=(-1)^r\binom{M+r-1}{r},
 \qquad r\in\mathbb N_0,
\]
gives $M+r-1=n+p(k+1)$. Only $r\ge0$ contributes, so the resulting
sum is exactly \eqref{eq:B}, with the stated upper limit.
For $n<q$, the coefficients are the entries of $H^{-1}$.
Multiplication of \eqref{eq:GF} by its denominator gives the right
recurrence, including the boundary convention $B_n=0$ for $n<0$.
For the left components, the reversed symbol is also $(1+z)^m$,
and the left generating function in
Theorem~\ref{thm:general-toeplitz-polynomials} is
\[
 \sum_{n\in\mathbb N_0}A_n^{(a)}(x)z^n
 =\frac{z^{a-1}(1+z)^{p-a}}{(1+z)^m-xz^p}
 =\sum_{k\in\mathbb N_0}x^kz^{pk+a-1}(1+z)^{-mk-q-a}.
\]
Now $r=n-a+1-pk$ and $M=mk+q+a$, so $M+r-1=n+q(k+1)$.
The same coefficient identity gives \eqref{eq:A}. Its first $p$
coefficients are the rows of $K^{-1}$, and multiplication by the
denominator gives $AT=xA$.
The last binomial coefficient in each nonempty sum is positive, so its
leading coefficient is nonzero.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- corollary-9: body_md -->
\label{prop:favard}
There is a unique $q\times p$ matrix $\Psi$ of positive finite measures,
supported in $[0,c]$, such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:moments}
 \int_{\mathbb R} x^\ell\,\mathrm d\Psi(x)=H E_qT^\ell E_p^{\mathsf T}K,
 \qquad \ell\in\mathbb N_0.
\end{equation}
Here $E_r=[I_r\ 0]$ selects the first $r$ coordinates.
Positivity means that each scalar entry $\Psi_{b,a}$ is a positive
measure. This definition applies to every rectangular size $q\times p$.
The associated biorthogonality is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:biorthogonality}
 \int_{\mathbb R} B_n(x)\,\mathrm d\Psi(x)\,A_l(x)=\delta_{n,l}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-9 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
Apply Corollary~\ref{prop:general-favard} to the bounded PBF
\eqref{eq:pbf} and its just-computed initial matrices. This gives
positivity, the moment identities, biorthogonality, and uniqueness.
For the symbol $(1+z)^m$ all root parameters equal one, so
Theorem~\ref{thm:general-support} gives $\lambda_-=0$, $z_+=q/p$ and
$\lambda_+=a(q/p)=c$. Its support bound
\eqref{eq:general-support-inclusion} places $\Psi$ in $[0,c]$.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- theorem-11: body_md -->
\label{thm:resolvent}
For $|\lambda|$ sufficiently large, the entries of
$R(\lambda)=(\lambda I-T)^{-1}$ satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:R}
 R_{i,j}(\lambda)=\gamma(\lambda)
 \sum_{k=0}^{\min(i,j)}h_{j-k}(S)h_{i-k}(\mathcal Z^{-1}),
 \qquad i,j\in\mathbb N_0.
\end{equation}
In particular, the Stieltjes matrix of Corollary~\ref{prop:favard} is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:W}
 W(\lambda):=\int_{\mathbb R}\frac{\mathrm d\Psi(x)}{\lambda-x}
 =H[R_{i,j}(\lambda)]_{\substack{i\in\{0,\dots,q-1\}\\j\in\{0,\dots,p-1\}}}K.
\end{equation}
Its continuation to $\mathbb C\setminus[0,c]$ is algebraic.
<!-- end theorem-11 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
Specialize Theorem~\ref{thm:general-resolvent} to $P(z)=(1+z)^m$,
whose constant and leading coefficients are one. Its scalar factor
$\Gamma$ becomes $\gamma$, yielding \eqref{eq:R} and \eqref{eq:W}.
The roots are algebraic functions of $\lambda$, so each finite
expression is algebraic. The measure support in $[0,c]$ supplies the
Stieltjes transform's analytic continuation to its complement.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- remark-18: body_md -->
Formula~\eqref{eq:W} identifies the normalized upper-left block of
the operator resolvent with the Stieltjes transform of $\Psi$ for
sufficiently large $|\lambda|$. The compactly supported measure
extends this Stieltjes transform analytically to
$\mathbb C\setminus[0,c]$. Its normalization is fixed by the matrix
moments in \eqref{eq:moments}.
<!-- end remark-18 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:roots}
For $0<x<c$, the roots of $Q(z;x)$ contain a unique middle conjugate
pair $\zeta,\bar\zeta$, with $\operatorname{Im}\zeta<0$, having exactly $q-1$
roots of smaller modulus and $p-1$ of larger modulus. The pair is
parametrized by
\begin{align}
 \zeta(\theta)&=\frac{\sin(q\theta)}{\sin(p\theta)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}m\theta},
 \label{eq:alpha}\\
 x(\theta)&=\frac{\sin^m(m\theta)}
 {\sin^p(p\theta)\sin^q(q\theta)},
 \qquad 0<\theta<\frac\pi m.\label{eq:trig}
\end{align}
The function $x(\theta)$ decreases strictly from $c$ to zero. The
small-root set continued from the upper half-plane has boundary value
$S_+=\mathcal S_0\cup\{\zeta\}$, where $\mathcal S_0$ is the set of $q-1$ smaller roots.
The large-root set has boundary value $\mathcal Z_+=\mathcal Z_0\cup\{\bar\zeta\}$,
where $\mathcal Z_0$ is the set of $p-1$ larger roots.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
The identity
\[
 1+\zeta(\theta)=
 \frac{\sin(m\theta)}{\sin(p\theta)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}q\theta}
\]
proves \eqref{eq:trig}. The only finite critical values of $a(z)$ are
$0$ and $c$: away from $z=-1,0$,
\[
 a'(z)=a(z)\frac{pz-q}{z(1+z)}.
\]
The parametrized $\zeta$ is nonreal in the open parameter interval,
and $\zeta'(\theta)\ne0$ because its argument has derivative $-m$.
Thus $x'(\theta)=a'(\zeta)\zeta'\ne0$. The endpoint limits
give strict decrease.

For this symbol, Theorem~\ref{thm:general-support} gives
$\lambda_-=0$, $z_+=q/p$ and $\lambda_+=c$, together with the
separation of the middle conjugate pair on $(0,c)$.
As $\theta\downarrow0$, the displayed $\zeta(\theta)$ tends to
$q/p$ with negative imaginary part. It is therefore the lower member
of the middle pair near $c$. Simplicity and separation of this pair
extend the identification throughout $(0,c)$. The boundary orientation
proved after that theorem places it in $S_+$.
<!-- end proof-36 -->
<!-- theorem-12: body_md -->
\label{thm:measure}
The measure of Corollary~\ref{prop:favard} is purely absolutely
continuous, with the explicit density
\begin{equation}\label{eq:weight}
 \mathrm d\Psi(x)=\rho(x)
 \begin{bmatrix}f_1(x)\\\vdots\\f_q(x)\end{bmatrix}
 \begin{bmatrix}g_1(x)&\cdots&g_p(x)\end{bmatrix}\mathrm d x,
 \qquad 0<x<c.
\end{equation}
The scalar density $\rho$ is positive in the open interval;
$f_b,g_a$ are real and nonnegative there. The matrix density has rank
exactly one at every interior point, and $\operatorname{supp}\Psi=[0,c]$.
<!-- end theorem-12 -->
<!-- proof-37: body_md -->
Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:general-measure} to the bounded PBF
\eqref{eq:pbf}. Its initial matrices are \eqref{eq:boundary}.
The scalar density in \eqref{eq:general-density-data} is exactly
\eqref{eq:rho}. It remains to evaluate the two matrix-vector products
that define $f,g$ for these binomial initial matrices.

The normalization by $H,K$ uses the identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:h-shift}
 h_k(1+y_1,\ldots,1+y_r)
 =\sum_{i=0}^k\binom{r+k-1}{r+i-1}h_i(y_1,\ldots,y_r).
\end{equation}
To prove this identity, use the generating function
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \prod_{j=1}^{r}(1-(1+y_j)t)^{-1}
 &=(1-t)^{-r}\prod_{j=1}^{r}
       \left(1-\frac{y_jt}{1-t}\right)^{-1}\\
 &=\sum_{i\in\mathbb N_0}h_i(y_1,\ldots,y_r)t^i(1-t)^{-r-i}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
The coefficient of $t^k$ on the last line is
$\sum_{i=0}^k\binom{r+k-1}{k-i}h_i(y_1,\ldots,y_r)$,
which is \eqref{eq:h-shift}.
For the column density vector, $\widehat{\mathcal Z}$ has $p+1$ roots and
the coefficient $\binom{p+i}{p+j}$ is $H_{i,j}$. Thus multiplication
by $H$ replaces each variable $v^{-1}$ by $1+v^{-1}$.
For the row density vector, $\widehat{\mathcal S}$ has $q+1$ roots and
$K_{i,j}=\binom{q+j}{q+i}$ performs the replacement $u\mapsto1+u$.
This gives \eqref{eq:f}--\eqref{eq:weight} from the general density formula.

Absolute continuity, positivity, rank one, and exact support
$[0,c]$ now follow from Theorem~\ref{thm:general-measure}.
<!-- end proof-37 -->
<!-- theorem-13: body_md -->
\label{thm:hardedge}
Put $\phi=\pi/m$, and define positive constants
\[
 F_b=\prod_{j=1}^{b-1}\frac{\sin((p+j)\phi)}{\sin(j\phi)},\qquad
 G_a=\prod_{j=1}^{a-1}\frac{\sin((q+j)\phi)}{\sin(j\phi)}.
\]
Then
\begin{align}
 \rho(x)&=\frac{\sin(q\phi)}{\pi}x^{1/m}
 [1+\mathrm O(x^{1/m})],&&x\downarrow0,\label{eq:rho-hard}\\
 f_b(x)&=F_bx^{(b-1)/m}[1+\mathrm O(x^{1/m})],&&x\downarrow0,\label{eq:f-hard}\\
 g_a(x)&=G_ax^{(a-1)/m}[1+\mathrm O(x^{1/m})],&&x\downarrow0.\label{eq:g-hard}
\end{align}
Consequently every entry has a nonzero leading term:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:entry-hard}
 \frac{\mathrm d\Psi_{b,a}}{\mathrm d x}(x)
 =\frac{\sin(q\phi)}{\pi}F_bG_a\,
 x^{(a+b-1)/m}[1+\mathrm O(x^{1/m})],\qquad x\downarrow0.
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-13 -->
<!-- proof-38: body_md -->
Set $\upsilon=x^{1/m}$ and write $z=-1+\upsilon w$.
The root equation becomes
\[
 w^m=(-1+\upsilon w)^q.
\]
At $\upsilon=0$ its roots are simple roots of $(-1)^q$; the implicit
function theorem gives analytic expansions in $\upsilon$.
The middle root has
$1+\zeta=\upsilon \mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}q\phi}+\mathrm O(\upsilon^2)$ as
$\upsilon\downarrow0$.
The product in \eqref{eq:rho} tends to one, proving
\eqref{eq:rho-hard}.

The rescaled quantities $(1+u)/\upsilon$, for $u\in\widehat{\mathcal S}$,
tend, as $\upsilon\downarrow0$, to the $q+1$ points
$\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(-q+2j)\phi}$, $j\in\{0,\dots,q\}$. This follows either from
\eqref{eq:alpha} or from ordering
$|-1+\upsilon w|=1-\upsilon\operatorname{Re}w
+\mathrm O(\upsilon^2)$ as $\upsilon\downarrow0$.
Likewise $(1+v^{-1})/\upsilon$, for $v\in\widehat{\mathcal Z}$, tend,
as $\upsilon\downarrow0$, to
$\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(-p+2j)\phi}$, $j\in\{0,\dots,p\}$.
Homogeneity and the finite geometric-progression identity
\[
 h_k(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}r\phi},\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(-r+2)\phi},\ldots,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}r\phi})
 =\prod_{j=1}^k\frac{\sin((r+j)\phi)}{\sin(j\phi)}
\]
give \eqref{eq:f-hard} and \eqref{eq:g-hard}. In the ranges used here,
all numerator and denominator angles lie strictly between zero and
$\pi$, so the constants are positive. Multiplication proves
\eqref{eq:entry-hard}.
<!-- end proof-38 -->
<!-- proposition-11: body_md -->
\label{prop:softedge}
Let $\mathcal S_{0,c}$ be the limiting smaller-root multiset at $x=c$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:softedge}
 \rho(x)=\frac1\pi\left(\prod_{u\in \mathcal S_{0,c}}|u|\right)
 \sqrt{\frac{2mq}{c p^3}}\sqrt{c-x}\,[1+\mathrm O(\sqrt{c-x})],\quad x\uparrow c.
\end{equation}
The functions $f_b,g_a$ remain bounded at $c$, and all matrix entries
are $\mathrm O(\sqrt{c-x})$ as $x\uparrow c$.
<!-- end proposition-11 -->
<!-- proof-39: body_md -->
At $z_*=q/p$, direct differentiation gives
$a''(z_*)=cp^3/(mq)>0$. The inverse expansion of $a$ gives
$-\operatorname{Im}\zeta(x)=\sqrt{2/a''(z_*)}\sqrt{c-x}
+\mathrm O(c-x)$ as $x\uparrow c$. The other roots stay nonzero and
vary analytically, proving
\eqref{eq:softedge}. The remaining assertions follow from the finite
expressions \eqref{eq:f}--\eqref{eq:g}.
<!-- end proof-39 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:q-vandermonde-pfaff}
For $p,q,i,j\in\mathbb N_0$ and $m=p+q$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:q-vandermonde-pfaff}
 \sum_{k\in\mathbb N_0}\tau^{(i-k)(j-k)}
 \genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{p}{i-k}_{\!\tau}\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{q}{j-k}_{\!\tau}\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{m+k}{k}_{\!\tau}
 =\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{i+q}{j}_{\!\tau}\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{j+p}{i}_{\!\tau}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-40: body_md -->
Suppose $i\le j$ and put $d=j-i$. If $d>q$, both sides vanish.
Assume $d\in\{0,\dots,q\}$, and set $r=i-k$. After factoring out
the $r=0$ term, the left-hand side is
\[
 \genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{q}{d}_{\!\tau}\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{m+i}{i}_{\!\tau}
 {}_3\phi_2\!\left[
 \begin{matrix}\tau^{-p},\tau^{-(q-d)},\tau^{-i}\\
 \tau^{d+1},\tau^{-(m+i)}\end{matrix};\tau,\tau\right].
\]
The series notation in this calculation means the terminating sum
\[
 {}_3\phi_2\!\left[\begin{matrix}a,b,\tau^{-N}\\c,d\end{matrix};\tau,z\right]
 =\sum_{r=0}^{N}
 \frac{(a;\tau)_r(b;\tau)_r(\tau^{-N};\tau)_r}
 {(c;\tau)_r(d;\tau)_r(\tau;\tau)_r}z^r,
\]
when its displayed denominators do not vanish.
The terminating $\tau$-Pfaff--Saalsch\"utz identity used here is
\[
 {}_3\phi_2\!\left[
 \begin{matrix}a,b,\tau^{-N}\\c,ab\tau^{1-N}/c\end{matrix};\tau,\tau\right]
 =\frac{(c/a;\tau)_N(c/b;\tau)_N}
 {(c;\tau)_N(c/(ab);\tau)_N}
\]
\cite{GasperRahman}. With
$a=\tau^{-p}$, $b=\tau^{-(q-d)}$, $c=\tau^{d+1}$, and $N=i$,
the second denominator parameter is
$ab\tau^{1-N}/c=\tau^{-(m+i)}$, and the sum terminates at or before
$r=i$. Thus the identity applies to the displayed series and gives
\[
 \genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{q}{d}_{\!\tau}\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{m+i}{i}_{\!\tau}
 \frac{(\tau^{p+d+1};\tau)_i(\tau^{q+1};\tau)_i}
 {(\tau^{d+1};\tau)_i(\tau^{m+1};\tau)_i}
 =\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{i+q}{i+d}_{\!\tau}\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{i+d+p}{i}_{\!\tau}.
\]
This is the right-hand side. The case $i>j$ follows by interchanging
$(i,p)$ with $(j,q)$, and $\tau=1$ by continuity.
<!-- end proof-40 -->
<!-- proposition-12: body_md -->
\label{prop:q-binomial-family}
Let $p,q\in\mathbb N$, $m=p+q$, $\alpha>0$, $\tau>0$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:q-symbol}
 P_\tau(z)=\prod_{\nu=0}^{m-1}(1+\alpha\tau^\nu z)
 =(-\alpha z;\tau)_m.
\end{equation}
Let $T_\tau$ be the $(p,q)$-banded Toeplitz matrix with symbol
$z^{-q}P_\tau(z)$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:q-binomial-entries}
 (T_\tau)_{i,j}=\alpha^h\tau^{\binom h2}\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{m}{h}_{\!\tau},
 \qquad h=q+i-j,
\end{equation}
where the right-hand side is zero for $h\notin\{0,\dots,m\}$.

For $a\in\{0,\dots,p-1\}$ and $b\in\{0,\dots,q-1\}$, define unit bidiagonal matrices by
\begin{align}
 (L_a)_{n,n-1}
 &=\alpha\tau^{q+a}\frac{1-\tau^n}{1-\tau^{n+q}},
 &&n\in\mathbb N,\label{eq:q-La}\\
 (U_b)_{n,n+1}
 &=\alpha^{-1}\tau^{-b}
   \frac{1-\tau^{n+1}}{1-\tau^{n+p+1}},
 &&n\in\mathbb N_0,\label{eq:q-Ub}
\end{align}
and let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:q-D}
 D_{n,n}=\alpha^q\tau^{\binom q2}
 \frac{(\tau^{n+p+1};\tau)_q}{(\tau^{n+1};\tau)_q}.
\end{equation}
Then the bounded PBF is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:q-PBF}
 T_\tau=L_0L_1\cdots L_{p-1}\,D\,U_{q-1}U_{q-2}\cdots U_0.
\end{equation}
The lower factors are ordered by increasing index from left to right;
the upper factors by increasing index from right to left, so $U_0$
is the rightmost factor. Factors within either full block commute.
Their partial products, which determine the initial-condition
matrices, depend on this specified order.

The associated initial-condition matrices and their inverses are
\begin{align}
 H_{i,j}&=\alpha^{i-j}\tau^{\binom{i-j}{2}}
 \genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{p+i}{i-j}_{\!\tau},&&i\in\{0,\dots,q-1\},\ j\in\{0,\dots,i\},
 \label{eq:q-H}\\
 (H^{-1})_{i,j}&=(-\alpha)^{i-j}\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{p+i}{i-j}_{\!\tau},
 &&i\in\{0,\dots,q-1\},\ j\in\{0,\dots,i\},\label{eq:q-Hinv}\\
 K_{i,j}&=\alpha^{-(j-i)}
 \tau^{-(j-i)(q+i)-\binom{j-i}{2}}\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{q+j}{j-i}_{\!\tau},
 &&j\in\{0,\dots,p-1\},\ i\in\{0,\dots,j\},\label{eq:q-K}\\
 (K^{-1})_{i,j}&=(-\alpha^{-1})^{j-i}
 \tau^{-(j-i)(q+i)}\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{q+j}{j-i}_{\!\tau},
 &&j\in\{0,\dots,p-1\},\ i\in\{0,\dots,j\}.\label{eq:q-Kinv}
\end{align}
All omitted triangular entries are zero.

With initial matrices $K^{-1},H^{-1}$ as in
\eqref{eq:general-initial-polynomials}, for $b\in\{1,\dots,q\}$ the right
polynomials satisfy
\begin{align}
 \sum_{n\in\mathbb N_0}B_n^{(b)}(x)z^n
 &=\frac{z^{b-1}(-\alpha\tau^{p+b}z;\tau)_{q-b}}
 {P_\tau(z)-xz^q},\label{eq:q-B-GF}\\
 B_n^{(b)}(x)
 &=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor(n-b+1)/q\rfloor}
 x^k[z^{n-b+1-qk}]
 \frac1{(-\alpha z;\tau)_{p+b}(-\alpha z;\tau)_m^k}.
 \label{eq:q-B-coeff}
\end{align}
For $a\in\{1,\dots,p\}$, put
\[
 t_p=\alpha^m\tau^{\binom m2},\qquad
 P_\tau^\vee(z)=z^mP_\tau(1/z)
 =t_p(-\alpha^{-1}z;\tau^{-1})_m.
\]
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:q-A-GF}
 \sum_{n\in\mathbb N_0}A_n^{(a)}(x)z^n
 =\frac{t_pz^{a-1}
 (-\alpha^{-1}\tau^{-(q+a)}z;\tau^{-1})_{p-a}}
 {P_\tau^\vee(z)-xz^p}.
\end{equation}
At $\tau=1$, denote the present matrix and polynomial families by
$T_\alpha$, $B_{n,\alpha}^{(b)}$ and $A_{n,\alpha}^{(a)}$, and use
the subscript $\mathrm{bin}$ for the normalized binomial family in
\eqref{eq:T}, \eqref{eq:B} and \eqref{eq:A}. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:tau-one-matrix-scale}
 T_\alpha=\alpha^qD_\alpha T_{\rm bin}D_\alpha^{-1},
 \qquad D_\alpha=\operatorname{diag}[\alpha^n]_{n\in\mathbb N_0}.
\end{equation}
This identity holds entrywise in the semi-infinite setting.
On every finite leading section it is a diagonal similarity
together with multiplication by $\alpha^q$.
The corresponding polynomial formulas are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:tau-one-polynomial-scale}
 \begin{aligned}
 B_{n,\alpha}^{(b)}(x)
 &=\alpha^{n-b+1}B_{n,\rm bin}^{(b)}(x/\alpha^q),
 &&b\in\{1,\dots,q\},\\
 A_{n,\alpha}^{(a)}(x)
 &=\alpha^{a-1-n}A_{n,\rm bin}^{(a)}(x/\alpha^q),
 &&a\in\{1,\dots,p\},
 \end{aligned}
 \qquad n\in\mathbb N_0.
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-12 -->
<!-- proof-41: body_md -->
The finite $\tau$-binomial theorem gives
\[
 (-\alpha z;\tau)_m=\sum_{h=0}^m
 \alpha^h\tau^{\binom h2}\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{m}{h}_{\!\tau}z^h,
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:q-binomial-entries}. Define weighted shifts
$\mathcal N,\mathcal M$ by
\[
 \mathcal N_{n,n-1}=\frac{1-\tau^n}{1-\tau^{n+q}},
 \qquad
 \mathcal M_{n,n+1}=\frac{1-\tau^{n+1}}
 {1-\tau^{n+p+1}}.
\]
Then $L_a=I+\alpha\tau^{q+a}\mathcal N$ and
$U_b=I+\alpha^{-1}\tau^{-b}\mathcal M$, proving the commutation
claim. Set $\mathcal L:=L_0\cdots L_{p-1}$ and
$\mathcal U:=U_{q-1}\cdots U_0$. Another application of the finite
$\tau$-binomial theorem gives
\[
 \mathcal L=\sum_{r=0}^{p}\alpha^r\tau^{qr+\binom r2}
   \genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{p}{r}_{\!\tau}\mathcal N^r,\qquad
 \mathcal U=\sum_{s=0}^{q}\alpha^{-s}\tau^{-qs+\binom{s+1}{2}}
   \genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{q}{s}_{\!\tau}\mathcal M^s.
\]
The powers of the weighted shifts have entries
\[
 (\mathcal N^{i-k})_{i,k}
 =\frac{(\tau^{k+1};\tau)_{i-k}}
        {(\tau^{k+q+1};\tau)_{i-k}},\qquad
 (\mathcal M^{j-k})_{k,j}
 =\frac{(\tau^{k+1};\tau)_{j-k}}
        {(\tau^{k+p+1};\tau)_{j-k}},
\]
for $k\in\{0,\dots,\min(i,j)\}$. Each identity is the product of the entries
along the unique path of that shift. For $r=i-k$ and $s=j-k$,
substitution of these ratios and of $D_{k,k}$ cancels the factors
with endpoints $k+p$ and $k+q$. The corresponding product
$\mathcal L_{i,k}D_{k,k}\mathcal U_{k,j}$ is
\[
 \alpha^h\tau^{\binom h2}
 \frac{(\tau;\tau)_m(\tau;\tau)_i(\tau;\tau)_j}
 {(\tau;\tau)_{i+q}(\tau;\tau)_{j+p}}
 \tau^{(i-k)(j-k)}
 \genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{p}{i-k}_{\!\tau}\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{q}{j-k}_{\!\tau}\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{m+k}{k}_{\!\tau}.
\]
Summing in $k$ and using Lemma~\ref{lem:q-vandermonde-pfaff}
reduces the last factors to $\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{m}{h}_{\!\tau}$, proving
\eqref{eq:q-PBF}. For positivity, the numerator and denominator of
each ratio in \eqref{eq:q-La}--\eqref{eq:q-D} have the same sign
when $\tau\ne1$. At $\tau=1$, a ratio
$(1-\tau^n)/(1-\tau^{n+r})$ has the positive limit $n/(n+r)$.
For fixed $\tau>0$ and $r\in\mathbb N$, this ratio tends, as
$n\to\infty$, to $1$ for $\tau\le1$ and to $\tau^{-r}$ for $\tau>1$.
The diagonal entries are finite products of such ratios or their
inverses, times a positive constant. Each sequence of allowed entries
therefore converges to a finite positive limit. Its finitely many
initial entries are also positive, so the entire sequence is bounded
above and away from zero. This proves the claimed bounded PBF.

For the initial-condition matrices, the products in
\eqref{eq:raw-H-definition}--\eqref{eq:normalized-boundary-definition},
with the present zero-based factor indices, are
\[
 \widetilde K_{i,j}=e_i^{\mathsf T}L_0\cdots L_{j-1}e_0,\qquad
 \widetilde H_{i,j}=e_0^{\mathsf T}U_{i-1}\cdots U_0e_j.
\]
For $\widetilde K_{i,j}$, the finite binomial expansion of the first
$j$ lower factors selects the coefficient of $\mathcal N^i$ and the
entry $(\mathcal N^i)_{i,0}$. For $\widetilde H_{i,j}$, the corresponding
expansion of the last $i$ upper factors selects $\mathcal M^j$ and
$(\mathcal M^j)_{0,j}$. The power formulas above give
\[
 \widetilde K_{i,j}=\alpha^i\tau^{qi+\binom i2}
 \genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{j}{i}_{\!\tau}\frac{(\tau;\tau)_i}{(\tau^{q+1};\tau)_i},\qquad
 \widetilde H_{i,j}=\alpha^{-j}\tau^{-ij+\binom{j+1}{2}}
 \genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{i}{j}_{\!\tau}\frac{(\tau;\tau)_j}{(\tau^{p+1};\tau)_j}.
\]
Divide the first expression by its value at $i=j$ to normalize each
column, and the second by its value at $j=i$ to normalize each row.
The powers of $\alpha$ become $\alpha^{-(j-i)}$ and $\alpha^{i-j}$.
For the powers of $\tau$, the simplifications are
\[
 \binom i2-\binom j2-q(j-i)
 =-(j-i)(q+i)-\binom{j-i}{2},\qquad
 \binom i2-ij+\binom{j+1}{2}=\binom{i-j}{2}.
\]
The Pochhammer ratios combine with the Gaussian coefficients to give
\eqref{eq:q-H}--\eqref{eq:q-K}.

To verify the inverses, multiply the proposed triangular matrices.
Use
\[
 \genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{N}{r}_{\!\tau}\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{N-r}{d-r}_{\!\tau}
 =\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{N}{d}_{\!\tau}\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{d}{r}_{\!\tau},
 \qquad d\in\{0,\dots,N\},\quad r\in\{0,\dots,d\},
\]
which follows by cancelling their defining Pochhammer products.
For every allowed $d\in\mathbb N_0$, the resulting entries are
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 (HH^{-1})_{k+d,k}
 &=(-\alpha)^d\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{p+k+d}{d}_{\!\tau}(1;\tau)_d,\\
 (KK^{-1})_{i,i+d}
 &=\alpha^{-d}\tau^{-d(q+i)-\binom d2}
   \genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{q+i+d}{d}_{\!\tau}(1;\tau)_d.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Here the finite sums have been evaluated using
\[
 \sum_{r=0}^{d}(-1)^r\tau^{\binom r2}\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{d}{r}_{\!\tau}
 =(1;\tau)_d.
\]
For $d=0$ every factor is one; for $d>0$ the product $(1;\tau)_d$
contains $1-\tau^0=0$. Thus both products are identity matrices,
which verifies the asserted inverses.

Finally,
\[
 \frac{z^{b-1}(-\alpha\tau^{p+b}z;\tau)_{q-b}}
 {P_\tau(z)}=\frac{z^{b-1}}{(-\alpha z;\tau)_{p+b}}.
\]
The reciprocal $\tau$-binomial theorem gives the coefficient of
$z^n$ in this expression as
$(-\alpha)^{n-b+1}\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{p+n}{n-b+1}_{\!\tau}$ when $n\ge b-1$, and zero
otherwise. For $n<q$ this is the entry $(n,b-1)$ of $H^{-1}$.
Adding $-xz^q$ to the denominator preserves those initial
coefficients, and multiplication by that denominator gives the right
recurrence by comparison of the coefficients of $z^{n+q}$.
This proves \eqref{eq:q-B-GF}. Expanding geometrically in
$xz^q/P_\tau$ leaves, at power $x^k$, the reciprocal product
$[(-\alpha z;\tau)_{p+b}(-\alpha z;\tau)_m^k]^{-1}$ and the factor
$z^{b-1+qk}$. Their coefficient of $z^n$ is
\eqref{eq:q-B-coeff}.

For the left family, the proposed quotient at $x=0$ reduces to
$z^{a-1}/(-\alpha^{-1}z;\tau^{-1})_{q+a}$. Its coefficient at $z^n$,
with $r=n-a+1\ge0$, is
\[
 (-\alpha^{-1})^r\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{q+n}{r}_{\!\tau^{-1}}
 =(-\alpha^{-1})^r\tau^{-r(q+a-1)}\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{q+n}{r}_{\!\tau},
\]
where the equality uses
$\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{N}{r}_{\!\tau^{-1}}
=\tau^{-r(N-r)}\genfrac{[}{]}{0pt}{}{N}{r}_{\!\tau}$.
For $n<p$ these are exactly the entries $(a-1,n)$ of $K^{-1}$.
Since the numerator in \eqref{eq:q-A-GF} has degree $p-1$,
multiplication by $P_\tau^\vee-xz^p$ and comparison at $z^{n+p}$
gives the left recurrence. Its nonzero extreme coefficient $t_p$
ensures uniqueness.

Finally, at $\tau=1$ the entries are
$\alpha^{q+i-j}\binom{m}{q+i-j}$, which proves
\eqref{eq:tau-one-matrix-scale}. Substituting $w=\alpha z$ in
\eqref{eq:q-B-GF}, and $w=z/\alpha$ in \eqref{eq:q-A-GF},
reduces the two generating functions to those of the binomial family
with spectral variable $x/\alpha^q$. Comparing coefficients gives
\eqref{eq:tau-one-polynomial-scale}.
<!-- end proof-41 -->
<!-- corollary-10: body_md -->
\label{cor:q-measure}
The unique Favard measure $\Psi_\tau$ satisfies
\[
 \int_{\mathbb R} x^\ell\mathrm d\Psi_\tau(x)
 =HE_qT_\tau^\ell E_p^{\mathsf T}K,
 \qquad
 \int_{\mathbb R} B_n\mathrm d\Psi_\tau A_l=\delta_{n,l}.
\]
Its endpoints are given by Theorem~\ref{thm:general-support}; its
exact support and rank-one density are given by Theorem~\ref{thm:general-measure} with
$\{\beta_\nu\}=\{\alpha\tau^\nu:\nu\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}\}$ and the explicit
$H,K$ in \eqref{eq:q-H}--\eqref{eq:q-K}. The measure is therefore
given by the algebraic root formula \eqref{eq:general-density-data}.
It is purely absolutely continuous on one interval.
For $\tau\ne1$ the scalar density $\rho$ vanishes as a square
root at both endpoints; at $\tau=1$ the lower endpoint is zero and has
the binomial behavior described above.
<!-- end corollary-10 -->
<!-- proof-42: body_md -->
Proposition~\ref{prop:q-binomial-family} shows that
\eqref{eq:q-PBF} is a PBF of $T_\tau$ with bounded factors and
computes its initial matrices \eqref{eq:q-H}--\eqref{eq:q-K}.
Corollary~\ref{prop:general-favard} therefore gives the unique
positive measure with the two displayed identities, in precisely this
normalization. The matrix $T_\tau$ belongs to the Toeplitz class of
Theorem~\ref{thm:toeplitz}, with root parameters $\alpha\tau^\nu$ and
$\omega=1$. Theorems~\ref{thm:general-support}
and~\ref{thm:general-measure} hold for every bounded PBF of such a
matrix; applied to \eqref{eq:q-PBF}, they give the endpoints, the
exact support, absolute continuity, and the rank-one density
\eqref{eq:general-density-data} formed with the present $H,K$.
For $\tau\ne1$ the root parameters are distinct, so
$\beta_q>\beta_{q+1}$ and Proposition~\ref{prop:general-edges} gives
square-root vanishing of $\rho$ at both endpoints. At $\tau=1$ the
scaling \eqref{eq:tau-one-matrix-scale} reduces the family to the binomial
one, whose behavior at zero is given by Theorem~\ref{thm:hardedge}.
<!-- end proof-42 -->
<!-- proposition-13: body_md -->
\label{prop:poisson-binomial-walk}
Let $T$ belong to the BTP Toeplitz class of
Theorem~\ref{thm:toeplitz}, with
$P(z)=\omega\prod_{\nu=1}^{m}(1+\beta_\nu z)$ and $m=p+q$.
For $r>0$, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:tilted-kernel}
 \mathsf P^{(r)}_{i,j}
 =\frac{r^{i-j}t_{i-j}}{a(r)},\qquad i,j\in\mathbb N_0,
 \qquad \pi_\nu(r)=\frac{\beta_\nu r}{1+\beta_\nu r}.
\end{equation}
Let $\mathsf X_{n,\nu}$ be independent Bernoulli variables with success
probabilities $\pi_\nu(r)$ and set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Bernoulli-walk}
 Y_{n+1}=Y_n+J_n,\qquad
 J_n=q-\sum_{\nu=1}^{m}\mathsf X_{n,\nu},\qquad
 \sigma_{\rm abs}=\inf\{n\in\mathbb N_0:Y_n<0\}.
\end{equation}
Starting from $i\in\mathbb N_0$, $\mathsf P^{(r)}$ is the transition matrix
of this walk killed at $\sigma_{\rm abs}$. Its jumps range from $-p$ to $q$.
Its row sums equal one for $i\ge p$, while for $i\in\{0,\dots,p-1\}$ the
missing mass is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:absorption-deficit}
 1-\sum_{j\in\mathbb N_0}\mathsf P^{(r)}_{i,j}
 =\mathbb P\!\left[\sum_{\nu=1}^{m}\mathsf X_{n,\nu}>q+i\right]>0.
\end{equation}
Adding an absorbing state with these transition probabilities gives a
stochastic matrix. Before absorption, the increment moments are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:walk-drift-variance}
 \mathbb E[J_n]=q-\mathcal F(r),\qquad
 \operatorname{Var}(J_n)
 =\sum_{\nu=1}^{m}\frac{\beta_\nu r}{(1+\beta_\nu r)^2}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-13 -->
<!-- proof-43: body_md -->
The probability-generating function of $X_n=\sum_\nu\mathsf X_{n,\nu}$ is
\[
 \mathbb E[s^{X_n}]
 =\prod_{\nu=1}^{m}\frac{1+\beta_\nu rs}{1+\beta_\nu r}
 =\frac{P(rs)}{P(r)}.
\]
Writing $h=q+i-j$, coefficient extraction gives
\[
 \mathbb P[J_n=j-i]
 =\frac{t_{i-j}r^{q+i-j}}{P(r)}
 =\mathsf P^{(r)}_{i,j}.
\]
Restricting the endpoint to $j\in\mathbb N_0$ proves the transition and deficit
formulas. Independence gives \eqref{eq:walk-drift-variance}.
<!-- end proof-43 -->
<!-- proposition-14: body_md -->
\label{prop:walk-spectral}
Let $A,B,\Psi$ be the polynomial families and measure for any of the ordered PBF
normalizations above. For all $i,j,n\in\mathbb N_0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:walk-KM}
 \mathbb P_i[Y_n=j,\ \sigma_{\rm abs}>n]
 =[\mathsf P^{(r)n}]_{i,j}
 =r^{i-j}\int_{\lambda_-}^{\lambda_+}
 \left(\frac{x}{a(r)}\right)^n B_i(x)\,\mathrm d\Psi(x)\,A_j(x).
\end{equation}
In particular, $\Psi_{1,1}$ has total mass one and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:walk-return-moments}
 \mathbb P_0[Y_n=0,\ \sigma_{\rm abs}>n]
 =\int_{\mathbb R}\left(\frac{x}{a(r)}\right)^n\mathrm d\Psi_{1,1}(x).
\end{equation}
For $|z|<1$, the generating functions for returns and for the first
return before absorption are
\begin{align}
 G_r(z)&=\int_{\mathbb R}\frac{\mathrm d\Psi_{1,1}(x)}{1-zx/a(r)},
 \label{eq:walk-return-Green}\\
 F_r(z)&=\mathbb E_0[z^{\sigma_0^+};\ \sigma_0^+<\sigma_{\rm abs}]
 =1-\frac1{G_r(z)},\qquad
 \sigma_0^+=\inf\{n\in\mathbb N:Y_n=0\}.
 \label{eq:walk-first-return}
\end{align}
<!-- end proposition-14 -->
<!-- proof-44: body_md -->
Repeated application of $TB=xB$ gives
$x^nB_i=\sum_k(T^n)_{i,k}B_k$. The sum is finite because $T$ is
banded. Multiply by $\mathrm d\Psi A_j$ and integrate; biorthogonality
then leaves only $k=j$, so
\[
 \int_{\mathbb R} x^nB_i(x)\,\mathrm d\Psi(x)\,A_j(x)=(T^n)_{i,j}.
\]
For a path $i=i_0,i_1,\ldots,i_n=j$ that stays in $\mathbb N_0$, the
product of its transition probabilities is
\[
 \prod_{h=0}^{n-1}\mathsf P^{(r)}_{i_h,i_{h+1}}
 =\frac{r^{i-j}}{a(r)^n}
   \prod_{h=0}^{n-1}T_{i_h,i_{h+1}}.
\]
Indeed, the exponent is the telescoping sum
$\sum_{h=0}^{n-1}(i_h-i_{h+1})=i-j$. Summing over the finitely
many paths with these endpoints gives
$[\mathsf P^{(r)n}]_{i,j}=r^{i-j}(T^n)_{i,j}/a(r)^n$ and proves
\eqref{eq:walk-KM} by a finite, entrywise calculation.

The initial polynomial vectors are
$B_0=[1,0,\ldots,0]$ and
$A_0=[1,0,\ldots,0]^{\mathsf T}$, which proves
\eqref{eq:walk-return-moments}. Since
$\lambda_+=\min_{r>0}a(r)\le a(r)$, geometric summation is justified
for $|z|<1$ and gives \eqref{eq:walk-return-Green}.
To obtain the first-return identity, put
$u_n=\mathbb P_0[Y_n=0,\sigma_{\rm abs}>n]$ and
$f_k=\mathbb P_0[\sigma_0^+=k<\sigma_{\rm abs}]$. For $n\in\mathbb N$,
decomposition at the first return and the Markov property give
\[
 u_n=\sum_{k=1}^n f_k u_{n-k},\qquad u_0=1.
\]
The generating functions converge absolutely for $|z|<1$.
Summing this convolution yields $G_r(z)=1+F_r(z)G_r(z)$.
In particular $G_r(z)\ne0$ there, and division gives
\eqref{eq:walk-first-return}.
<!-- end proof-44 -->
<!-- proposition-15: body_md -->
\label{prop:walk-centering}
The unique value of $r$ for which the mean increment is zero is $r=z_+$ of
Theorem~\ref{thm:general-support}. Its normalizing constant is
$a(z_+)=\lambda_+$. In the binomial case,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:centered-binomial}
 r_*=\frac qp,\qquad a(r_*)=c=\frac{m^m}{p^p q^q},\qquad
 X_n\sim\operatorname{Bin}(m,q/m),\qquad
 \operatorname{Var}(J_n)=\frac{pq}{m}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-15 -->
<!-- proof-45: body_md -->
The mean increment in \eqref{eq:walk-drift-variance} is
$q-\mathcal F(r)$, so it vanishes exactly when $\mathcal F(r)=q$.
The function $\mathcal F$ is strictly increasing from $0$ to $m$;
there is therefore exactly one such $r$, namely $z_+$.
Equation~\eqref{eq:a-critical} shows that $a'(r)<0$ below $z_+$
and $a'(r)>0$ above it. Thus $a(z_+)$ is its unique minimum,
which Theorem~\ref{thm:general-support} identifies with $\lambda_+$.

When $\beta_\nu=1$, the equation $mr/(1+r)=q$ reduces to $pr=q$.
Hence $r_*=q/p$ and each Bernoulli probability is
$r_*/(1+r_*)=q/m$. There are $m$ independent trials, so
$X_n$ has distribution $\operatorname{Bin}(m,q/m)$ and
$\operatorname{Var}(J_n)=\operatorname{Var}(X_n)=m(q/m)(p/m)=pq/m$.
Finally, substitution in $a(r)=(1+r)^m/r^q$ gives
$a(r_*)=m^m/(p^p q^q)$.
<!-- end proof-45 -->
<!-- proposition-16: body_md -->
\label{prop:walk-Doob}
Use the transition matrix of the centered walk $\widehat{\mathsf P}=\mathsf P^{(z_+)}$.
The bounded-jump centered walk has $\sigma_{\rm abs}<\infty$ almost surely.
Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:overshoot-harmonic}
 \mathfrak h_i=i-\mathbb E_i[Y_{\sigma_{\rm abs}}],\qquad i\in\mathbb N_0.
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:harmonic-bounds}
 i+1\le\mathfrak h_i\le i+p,\qquad
 \widehat{\mathsf P}\mathfrak h=\mathfrak h.
\end{equation}
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:conservative-transform}
 \mathsf P^\uparrow_{i,j}
 =\widehat{\mathsf P}_{i,j}\frac{\mathfrak h_j}{\mathfrak h_i}
\end{equation}
is a stochastic matrix on $\mathbb N_0$. Its $n$-step probabilities are
\eqref{eq:walk-KM}, with $r=z_+$, multiplied by
$\mathfrak h_j/\mathfrak h_i$.
<!-- end proposition-16 -->
<!-- proof-46: body_md -->
To prove almost sure absorption, fix an integer $N>i$ and stop when the walk first
leaves $\{0,\dots,N-1\}$; call this time $\sigma_N$. The increment
$-p$ has positive probability. A sufficiently long block of such
increments forces exit from this finite interval, from any of its
states. Independence bounds the probability of surviving successive
blocks by a geometric sequence, so $\sigma_N<\infty$ almost surely.
The centered walk is a martingale and its stopped values are bounded
between $-p$ and $N+q-1$. Optional stopping, first at
$\min(n,\sigma_N)$ and then by bounded convergence, gives
$\mathbb E_i[Y_{\sigma_N}]=i$.

Let $u_N$ be the probability of exit through the upper endpoint.
The exit value is at least $N$ on that event and at least $-p$
otherwise. Consequently
\[
 i\ge Nu_N-p(1-u_N),\qquad
 u_N\le\frac{i+p}{N+p}.
\]
If the walk never enters the negative half-line, it must exit each
finite interval through its upper endpoint. Hence
$\mathbb P_i[\sigma_{\rm abs}=\infty]\le u_N$ for every $N>i$, and letting
$N\to\infty$ proves $\sigma_{\rm abs}<\infty$ almost surely.

At absorption, $-p\le Y_{\sigma_{\rm abs}}\le-1$, because the preceding state
was nonnegative and each downward jump is at most $p$. This proves
finiteness and the bounds $i+1\le\mathfrak h_i\le i+p$. Put
$u(i)=\mathbb E_i[Y_{\sigma_{\rm abs}}]$ for $i\in\mathbb N_0$ and $u(j)=j$ for $j<0$.
The first-step decomposition gives $u(i)=\mathbb E_i[u(Y_1)]$,
while centering gives $i=\mathbb E_i[Y_1]$. Their difference, with
$\mathfrak h_j=0$ for $j<0$, proves
$\widehat{\mathsf P}\mathfrak h=\mathfrak h$.
As $\mathfrak h_i>0$, the transformed entries are nonnegative and
their row sums are
\[
 \sum_{j\in\mathbb N_0}\mathsf P^\uparrow_{i,j}
 =\frac1{\mathfrak h_i}\sum_{j\in\mathbb N_0}
      \widehat{\mathsf P}_{i,j}\mathfrak h_j=1.
\]
Along a finite path, the factors $\mathfrak h_{i_{h+1}}/\mathfrak h_{i_h}$
multiply to $\mathfrak h_j/\mathfrak h_i$. Summing the path weights
therefore gives
$[(\mathsf P^\uparrow)^n]_{i,j}
 =\mathfrak h_j[\widehat{\mathsf P}^{\,n}]_{i,j}/\mathfrak h_i$,
which is the asserted formula for its transition probabilities.
<!-- end proof-46 -->
